Therefore, we believe that the reference to a ‘hedging relationship of a type that does not qualify for hedge accounting’ in IFRS 1.B5 relates only to the qualifying criterion in IFRS 9.6.4.1(a) (i.e., eligible hedging instruments and hedged items) and the ‘conditions for hedge accounting’ in IFRS 1.B6 relate to the qualifying criteria in IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c). The discussions in sections 4.4 above to 4.7 below are based on this view. The Annual improvements to IFRS accounting standards issued in July 2024 clarified the above position. The amendments added a cross-reference to IFRS 9.6.4.1(a) from IFRS 1.B5 to clarify that the phrase ‘hedging relationship of a type that does not qualify for hedge accounting’ in paragraph B5 of IFRS 1 relates only to the ‘eligibility’ of a hedging relationship (i.e., there are eligible hedging instruments and eligible hedged items). Also, the phrase ‘conditions for hedge accounting’ in IFRS 1.B6 was replaced with ‘qualifying criteria for hedge accounting in IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c)’. The amendments to IFRS 1 must be applied for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2026 and early application is permitted. [IFRS 1(2026).B5, **IFRS 1(2026).B6, IFRS 9.6.4.1]** 4.4.2 Applicability of IAS 39 hedge requirements A transition requirement of IFRS 9 for hedge accounting permits an entity already applying IFRS accounting standards to choose as its accounting policy to continue to apply the hedge accounting requirements of IAS 39 Financial Instruments: Recognition and Measurement to all of its hedging relationships instead of the requirements in Chapter 6 of IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.7.2.21]. First-time adopters do not have this choice because IFRS 1 prohibits first-time adopters from applying the transition provisions in other IFRS accounting standards, except as specified in Appendices B-E. [IFRS 1.9]. Further, IFRS 1.B4-B6 contain mandatory exceptions for hedge accounting which do not refer to the transition requirements of IFRS 9, and the basis for conclusion attached to IFRS 9 under the heading “Transition related to the hedge accounting requirements” states “The IASB decided not to change the requirements of IFRS 1 for hedge accounting. The IASB noted that a first-time adopter would need to look at the entire population of possible hedging relationships and assess which ones would meet the qualifying criteria of the new hedge accounting model.” [IFRS 9.BC7.52]. How we see it Accordingly, a first-time adopter applies IFRS 9’s requirements on hedge accounting. This is in contrast to an entity already applying IFRS accounting standards, who would be permitted to continue to apply IAS 39 hedge accounting. Nevertheless, in our view, first-time adopters are permitted to apply IAS 39 hedge accounting to a fair value hedge of the interest rate exposure of a portfolio of financial assets or financial liabilities (and only to such a hedge) (see Chapter 49 at section 10.2). This is because this is permitted by Chapter 6 of IFRS 9, rather than being a transitional provision included in Chapter 7 of IFRS 9. First-time adoption 4.5 Hedge accounting in the opening IFRS statement of financial position 4.5.1 Measurement of derivatives and elimination of deferred gains and losses Under its previous GAAP, an entity’s accounting policies might have included a number of accounting treatments for derivatives that formed part of a hedging relationship. For example, accounting policies might have included those where the derivative was: • Not explicitly recognised as an asset or liability (e.g., in the case of a forward contract used to hedge an expected but uncontracted future transaction) • Recognised as an asset or liability but at an amount different from its fair value (e.g., a purchased option recognised at its original cost, perhaps less amortisation; or an interest rate swap accounted for by accruing the periodic interest payments and receipts) • Subsumed within the accounting for another asset or liability (e.g., a foreign currency denominated monetary item and a matching forward contract or swap accounted for as a ‘synthetic’ functional currency denominated monetary item) Whatever the previous accounting treatment, a first-time adopter must account for derivatives in the opening IFRS statement of financial position as assets or liabilities measured at fair value in accordance with IFRS 9 (for the treatment of embedded derivatives, see section 4.11 below). [IFRS 1.B4(a)]. All derivatives are measured at fair value through profit or loss, other than those that are financial guarantee contracts, a commitment to provide a loan at a below-market interest rate, a loan commitment that is subject to the impairment requirements of IFRS 9, or those that are designated and effective as hedging instruments. Accordingly, the difference between the previous carrying amount of these derivatives (which may have been zero) and their fair value is recognised as an adjustment of the balance of retained earnings at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.IG58A]. If an entity is unable to determine whether a particular portion of an adjustment is a transition adjustment (i.e., a change in accounting policy) or a change in estimate, it must treat that portion as a change in accounting estimate, with the appropriate disclosures in accordance with IAS 8. [IFRS 1.IG58B, IAS 8.36, IAS 8.37, IAS 8.39, IAS 8.40]. The distinction between changes in accounting policies and changes in accounting estimates is discussed in detail in Chapter 3 at section 4.2. Hedge accounting policies under an entity’s previous GAAP might also have included one or both of the following accounting treatments: • Derivatives were measured at fair value but, to the extent they were regarded as hedging future transactions, the gain (or loss) arising was reported as a liability (or asset) such as deferred (or accrued) income. • Realised gains or losses arising on the termination of a previously unrecognised derivative used in a hedging relationship (such as an interest rate swap hedging a borrowing) were included in the statement of financial position as deferred or accrued income and amortised over the remaining term of the hedged exposure. In all cases, an entity is required to eliminate deferred gains and losses arising on derivatives that were reported in accordance with previous GAAP as if they were assets or liabilities. [IFRS 1.B4(b)]. In contrast to adjustments made to restate derivatives at fair value, IFRS 1’s implementation guidance does not specify in general terms how to deal with adjustments to eliminate deferred gains or losses, i.e., whether they are taken to retained earnings or a separate component of equity. How we see it We believe that the requirement to eliminate deferred gains and losses does not extend to those that have been included in the carrying amount of other assets or liabilities that will continue to be recognised under IFRS accounting standards. For example, under an entity’s previous GAAP, the carrying amount of non-financial assets such as inventories or property, plant and equipment might have included the equivalent of a basis adjustment (i.e., hedging gains or losses were considered an integral part of the asset’s cost). Of course, entities also need to consider any other provisions of IFRS 1 that apply to those hedged items, e.g., whether any of the exemptions such as those for business combinations (see section 5.2 below) or deemed cost (see section 5.4 below) will be used. First-time adoption 4.5.2 Hedging relationships reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position IFRS 1 states that a first-time adopter must not reflect a hedging relationship in its opening IFRS statement of financial position if that hedging relationship is of a type that does not qualify for hedge accounting under IFRS 9 (see section 4.4.1 above). [IFRS 9.6.4.1(a)]. As examples of this, IFRS 1 cites many hedging relationships where the hedging instrument is a stand-alone written option or a net written option; or where the hedged item is a net position in a cash flow hedge for another risk than foreign currency risk. [IFRS 1.B5]. Previous GAAP hedge documentation and effectiveness that were not fully compliant with the criteria in IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c) do not mean that the hedging relationship is of a type that does not qualify for hedge accounting under IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.6.4.1(b), IFRS 9.6.4.1(c)]. (See section 4.6 below for requirements for post-transition date hedge accounting). However, if an entity designated a net position as a hedged item under its previous GAAP, it could designate as a hedged item under IFRS accounting standards an individual item within that net position, or a net position that meets the requirements in IFRS 9.6.6.1, provided that it does so no later than the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 9.6.6.1]. In other words, such designation would allow the hedging relationship to be reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. On the other hand, a hedging relationship designated under an entity’s previous GAAP is reflected in its opening IFRS statement of financial position if that hedging relationship consists of eligible hedging instruments and eligible hedged items in IFRS 9.6.4.1(a), regardless of whether or not the hedge designation, documentation and effectiveness criteria for hedge accounting under IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c) are met on the date of transition. [IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 9.6.4.1]. 4.5.2.A Prohibition on retrospective designation A first-time adopter is not permitted to designate hedges retrospectively in relation to transactions entered into before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.B6]. Instead, it must apply the requirements prospectively. In the Basis for Conclusions, it is explained that: “… it is unlikely that most entities would have adopted IAS 39’s criteria for (a) documenting hedges at their inception and (b) testing the hedges for effectiveness, even if they intended to continue the same hedging strategies after adopting IAS 39. Furthermore, retrospective designation of hedges (or retrospective reversal of their designation) could lead to selective designation of some hedges to report a particular result.” [IFRS 1.BC75]. While this paragraph refers to IAS 39’s criteria, the IASB has since reinforced the above basis for conclusion when it issued IFRS 9, which says the following in its Basis for Conclusions: “To the extent that an entity wants to apply hedge accounting, those hedging relationships should be documented on or before the transition date. This is consistent with the transition requirements for existing users of IFRS and the existing transition requirements of IFRS 1 …”. [IFRS 9.BC7.52]. 4.5.2.B Designation in anticipation of adoption of IFRS accounting standards If a first-time adopter, in anticipation of the adoption of IFRS accounting standards, decides to designate a transaction as a hedge under IFRS 9 and completes the required documentation sometime before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, some have questioned whether upon adoption of IFRS accounting standards, hedge accounting is applied prior to the date of transition. How we see it In our view, as long as the hedge is properly designated and documented in accordance with IFRS 9.6.4.1 prior to the date of transition, the first-time adopter applies hedge accounting from the date that it met the requirements. As explained in section 4.5.2.A above, a first-time adopter cannot designate a transaction retrospectively as a hedge under IFRS accounting standards. First-time adoption 4.5.3 Reflecting cash flow hedges in the opening IFRS statement of financial position A first-time adopter could have deferred gains and losses on a cash flow hedge of a forecast transaction under its previous GAAP. If, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the hedged forecast transaction is not highly probable, but is expected to occur, the entire deferred gain or loss is recognised in the cash flow hedge reserve within equity. [IFRS 1.IG60B]. This is consistent with the treatment required for deferred gains or losses on cash flow hedges applicable after the transition to IFRS accounting standards (see Chapter 49 at section 7.2.1 and section 8.3.2). [IFRS 9.6.5.11(d), IFRS 9.6.5.12(a)]. How we see it The standard is not explicit as to how an entity deals with such a hedge if, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the forecast transaction is highly probable. However, it would make no sense if the hedge of a transaction that is no longer highly probable but is still expected to occur were required to be reflected in the cash flow hedge reserve within equity in the opening IFRS statement of financial position as described above, but the hedge of the highly probable forecast transaction were not. Therefore, a cash flow hedge is reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position in the way set out above if the hedged item is a forecast transaction that is highly probable (see Illustration 4-2 below). It also follows that a cash flow hedge of the variability in cash flows attributable to a particular risk associated with a recognised asset or liability (such as all or some future interest payments on variable rate debt) is reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. To do otherwise would allow an entity to choose not to designate (in accordance with IFRS 9) certain cash flow hedges, say those that are in a loss position, until one day after its date of transition, thereby allowing associated hedging losses to bypass profit or loss completely. However, this would effectively result in the retrospective de-designation of hedges to achieve a desired result, thereby breaching the general principle of IFRS 1 (i.e., a first-time adopter cannot designate a hedging relationship retrospectively). If, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the forecast transaction was not expected to occur, consistent with the requirements of IFRS 9.6.5.6-6.5.7 and IFRS 9.6.5.12(b), a first-time adopter reclassifies any related deferred gains and losses that are not expected to be recovered into retained earnings. [IFRS 1.IG60B, IFRS 9.6.5.6, IFRS 9.6.5.7, IFRS 9.6.5.12(b)]. Illustration 4-2: Unrecognised gains and losses on existing cash flow hedge Entity A has the euro (€) as its functional currency. In September 20X1, it entered into a forward currency contract to sell US dollars (US$) for euros in twelve months to hedge US$ denominated sales that it forecasts are highly probable to occur in September 20X2. Entity A prepares its first IFRS financial statements and its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. The historical cost of the forward contract is €nil and at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards it had a positive fair value of €100. Case 1: Gains and losses deferred Under Entity A’s previous GAAP, until the sales occurred the forward contract was recognised in the statement of financial position at its fair value and the resulting gain or loss was deferred in the statement of financial position as a liability or an asset. When the sale occurred, any deferred gain or loss was recognised in profit or loss as an adjustment to the revenue recognised on the hedged sale. This relationship must be reflected in Entity A’s opening IFRS statement of financial position whether or not the hedge designation, documentation and effectiveness criteria for hedge accounting under IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c) are met on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards: the deferred gain is reclassified to the cash flow hedge reserve within equity and there is no adjustment to the carrying amount of the forward contract. [IFRS 1.IG60B]. Case 2: Gains and losses unrecognised Under Entity A’s previous GAAP, the contract was not recognised in the statement of financial position. When the sale occurred, any unrecognised gain or loss was recognised in profit or loss as an offset to the revenue recognised on the hedged sale. How we see it Although Case 2 is more problematic, we consider that it is accounted for in the same way as Case 1. The difference between the previous carrying amount of a derivative and its fair value would be recognised in the cash flow hedge reserve within equity. First-time adoption Many existing hedging relationships that were acceptable under previous GAAP do not meet the full hedge accounting requirements of IFRS 9.6.4.1 although the hedging relationships are of a type that qualify for hedge accounting under IFRS 9.6.4.1(a) and are therefore reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. Also, in many such cases hedge ineffectiveness has not previously been assessed or separately recognised in profit or loss. For cash flow hedges, ineffectiveness is not recognised in opening retained earnings but is deferred, as part of the cash flow hedge reserve within equity. As noted above, the entire unrecognised portion of the fair value of the hedging instrument is recognised in the cash flow hedge reserve in equity in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. There is no reference to the effective portion. [IFRS 1.IG60B]. 4.5.4 Reflecting fair value hedges in the opening IFRS statement of financial position If a first-time adopter has, under its previous GAAP, deferred or not recognised gains and losses on a fair value hedge of a hedged item that is not measured at fair value, the entity adjusts the carrying amount of the hedged item at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The adjustment, which is essentially the effective part of the hedge that was not recognised in the carrying amount of the hedged item under the previous GAAP, is calculated as the lower of: [IFRS 1.IG60A] (a) That portion of the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item that was not recognised under previous GAAP And (b) That portion of the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedging instrument which, under previous GAAP, was either (i) not recognised or (ii) deferred in the statement of financial position as an asset or liability Therefore, an entity could be required to recognise some ineffectiveness in retained earnings in the opening statement of financial position for fair value hedges, if the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item is lower than the unrecognised cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument. However, if the hedged item fair value adjustment on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is restricted by the change in fair value of the hedging instrument (i.e., where the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item is higher than the cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument), any ineffectiveness is not recognised on the date of transition. 4.5.5 Reflecting foreign currency net investment hedges in the opening IFRS statement of financial position IFRS 1 does not provide explicit guidance on reflecting foreign currency net investment hedges in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. How we see it Since IFRS 9 requires that ongoing IFRS reporting entities account for foreign currency net investment hedges similarly to cash flow hedges, it follows that the first-time adoption provisions regarding cash flow hedges (see section 4.5.3 above) also apply to hedges of foreign currency net investments. Specifically, the entire deferred or unrecognised gain or loss on the hedging instrument is recognised in the currency translation adjustment on transition. This will result in some previously deferred or unrecognised gains or losses on hedging instruments not being recognised in profit or loss until the foreign operation is disposed of. IFRS 1 allows a first-time adopter to apply the exemption to reset the cumulative translation difference for all foreign operations to zero (see section 5.6 below); however, the standard does not explicitly state that this exemption extends to the gain or loss on the hedging instruments for net investment hedges. How we see it We believe that the requirement to reset the cumulative translation differences also applies to related gains and losses on hedging instruments (see section 5.6.1 below). Therefore, a first-time adopter that applies the exemption to reset cumulative translation differences to zero recognises those pre-transition gains and losses on the hedging instruments in the opening balance of retained earnings to avoid a disparity between the treatment of the gains and losses on the hedged item and the hedging instrument. Those pre-transition gains and losses on the hedging instruments that were recognised in retained earnings will not be reclassified to profit or loss upon disposal of a foreign operation. First-time adoption 4.5.6 ‘Costs of hedging’ (where excluded from the hedging relationship under previous GAAP) The time value of options, forward points of forward contracts and foreign currency basis spreads (collectively referred to as ‘costs of hedging’) might not have been accounted for in the manner permitted by IFRS 9. For instance, they might have been amortised under previous GAAP, regardless of whether there was a transaction-related or a time-period related hedged item. Also, the amount deferred under previous GAAP might not represent the ‘aligned’ value of ‘costs of hedging’ if the critical terms of the hedging instrument are not fully aligned with those of the hedged item (see Chapter 49 at section 7.5.1.A). By separately accruing the option time value or forward points, for example, under previous GAAP and only locking in the intrinsic or spot value of the hedged item, an entity has made a clear intention that only the intrinsic or spot value forms part of the hedge. Therefore, the special cash flow hedging rules in IFRS 1.IG60B (see section 4.5.3 above) apply purely to the full unrecognised intrinsic or spot value. [IFRS 1.IG60B]. Similarly, if the ‘costs of hedging’ had been excluded from a fair value hedging relationship, they would not form part of the adjustment to the hedging instrument taken to retained earnings required by IFRS 1.IG60A (see section 4.5.4 above). [IFRS 1.IG60A]. How we see it IFRS 1 does not provide specific guidance on how to account for ‘costs of hedging’ excluded from a hedging relationship on transition to IFRS accounting standards. In our view, the same approach as applied to cash flow hedges (i.e., IFRS 1.IG60B) is also applied to the ‘costs of hedging’. Accordingly, any previously unrecognised amount of the fair value of the option time value, forward points or foreign currency basis spread would be recognised in a separate component of equity recognised at the date of transition. Therefore, the ‘costs of hedging’ excluded from the hedging relationship under previous GAAP are neither recognised in the cash flow hedge reserve nor in retained earnings. The same accounting applies to ‘costs of hedging’ excluded from cash flow and fair value hedging relationships. In many cases, the amount of ‘costs of hedging’ that was amortised or deferred under previous GAAP and is recognised as a separate component of equity on transition includes accumulated differences due to the different accounting treatments under previous GAAP compared to IFRS 9 (rather than, say, representing only the aligned time value of the option, forward element or foreign currency basis spread). Any such accumulated differences are included in the amount deferred under previous GAAP and recognised in equity. If the ‘costs of hedging’ were not excluded from the hedging relationship under previous GAAP, the above accounting does not apply but instead they give rise to ineffectiveness. The treatment of ineffectiveness for cash flow hedges, fair value hedges and foreign currency net investment hedges in the opening IFRS statement of financial position is explained in sections 4.5.3 to 4.5.5 above. 4.6 Hedge accounting: subsequent treatment IFRS 1’s implementation guidance explains that hedge accounting can be applied prospectively only from the date the hedging relationship is fully designated and documented. In other words, after the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, hedge accounting under IFRS 9 can be applied only if the qualifying criteria in IFRS 9.6.4.1 are met. [IFRS 9.6.4.1, IFRS 9.6.5.1]. Therefore, if the hedging instrument is still held at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the designation and documentation of the hedging relationship must be completed (and the effectiveness requirements met) on or before that date if the hedging relationship is to qualify for hedge accounting from that date. [IFRS 1.IG60]. For further discussion on measuring ineffectiveness, see Chapter 49 at section 7.4. 4.6.1 Discontinuation of a hedging relationship Before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards an entity might have designated as a hedge a transaction under previous GAAP that is of a type that qualifies for hedge accounting under IFRS 9, which consists only of eligible hedging instruments and eligible hedged items in IFRS 9.6.4.1(a), but does not meet other qualifying criteria in IFRS 9 (i.e., IFRS 9.6.4.1 (b)-(c)) at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 9.6.4.1]. In this case, the entity follows the general requirements in IFRS 9.6.5.6-6.5.7 for discontinuing hedge accounting subsequent to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards - these are dealt with in Chapter 49 at section 8.3. [IFRS 1.B6, IFRS 9.6.5.6, IFRS 9.6.5.7]. First-time adoption For cash flow hedges, any net cumulative gain or loss that was reclassified to the cash flow hedge reserve on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (see section 4.5.3 above) remains there until: **[IFRS 1.IG60B]** (a) The forecast transaction subsequently results in the recognition of a non-financial asset or non- financial liability (b) The forecast transaction affects profit or loss Or (c) Subsequently, circumstances change and the forecast transaction is no longer expected to occur, in which case any related net cumulative gain or loss that had been reclassified to the cash flow hedge reserve on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is reclassified to profit or loss The requirements above do little more than reiterate the general requirements of IFRS 9, i.e., that hedge accounting can only be applied prospectively if the qualifying criteria are met, and entities are expected to experience few interpretative problems in dealing with this aspect of the hedge accounting requirements. 4.6.2 Ineffectiveness of cash flow and foreign currency net investment hedges in continuing hedging relationships Hedge accounting in accordance with IFRS 9 is applied prospectively from the date the hedge accounting is fully designated and documented. [IFRS 1.IG60]. Accounting for ongoing cash flow hedges and foreign currency net investment hedges is discussed in more detail in Chapter 49 at sections 7.2 and 7.3. As noted in sections 4.5.3 and 4.5.5 above, ineffectiveness relating to cash flow and foreign currency net investment hedges reflected in the opening statement of financial position is not recognised in the opening retained earnings but is deferred, as part of the cash flow hedge reserve (or cumulative translation reserve) within equity. The entity does not recognise any past ineffectiveness deferred in equity on transition in profit or loss as ineffectiveness in subsequent periods. Rather, the entity measures ineffectiveness in subsequent periods by reference to cumulative changes in the fair value of the hedging instrument and hedged item subsequent to the date of transition (if that is the date from which the hedging relationship qualifies under IFRS 9). In order to capture the appropriate cumulative change in fair value of the hedged item, where a hypothetical derivative is used to measure ineffectiveness, the ineffectiveness recognised in profit or loss on the continuing hedging relationship is determined by reference to the hypothetical derivative where terms were set at inception of the hedging relationship, rather than on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. This means that the fair value movements arising on the hedged item which are used in calculating the ongoing adjustments required to the cash flow hedge reserve derive from the hypothetical derivative whose terms were set at the inception of the hedging relationship (designated under previous GAAP). Any ineffectiveness deferred in equity on transition (as part of, say, the cash flow hedge reserve) will remain in equity until (a) the forecast transaction subsequently results in the recognition of a non-financial asset or non-financial liability (when the amount deferred in equity is included directly in the initial cost or other carrying amount of the asset or liability), (b) the forecast transaction affects profit or loss, or (c) subsequently circumstances change and the forecast transaction is no longer expected to occur, in which case the amount is reclassified from equity to profit or loss. How we see it This deferral of ineffectiveness on transition implies a discrepancy between the profit or loss impact of the hedged item and the amounts reclassified from cash flow hedge reserves under IFRS 9.6.5.11(d) and IFRS 9.6.5.12. Therefore, when the hedged item affects several periods (e.g., a hedge of variable interest on a debt instrument), it is necessary to recognise this mismatch appropriately over the hedging period. For a net investment hedge, the amounts accumulated in the cumulative translation reserve are only reclassified to profit or loss on disposal or, in certain circumstances, partial disposal of the foreign operation in accordance with IAS 21 (see Chapter 16 at section 6.6 and Chapter 49 at section 7.3). In certain cases, no deferral of ineffectiveness will occur, (e.g., where on transition the change in fair value of the hedged item exceeds the change in fair value of the hedging instrument). In those circumstances, subsequent changes in fair value of the hedging instrument that are effective are deferred in the cash flow hedge reserve or for a foreign currency net investment hedge, the cumulative translation reserve. First-time adoption 4.6.3 Ineffectiveness of fair value hedges in continuing hedging relationships Hedge accounting in accordance with IFRS 9 is applied prospectively from the date the hedge accounting is fully designated and documented. [IFRS 1.IG60]. Accounting for ongoing fair value hedges is discussed in more detail in Chapter 49 at section 7.1. As noted in section 4.5.4 above, if the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item is lower than the unrecognised cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument, ineffectiveness relating to fair value hedges is recognised in retained earnings on transition. However, if the hedged item’s fair value adjustment on transition is restricted by the change in fair value of the hedging instrument (i.e., where the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item is higher than the cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument), any ineffectiveness is not recognised on transition. How we see it The ineffectiveness on a fair value hedge not recognised on transition is not subsequently recognised in profit or loss through the next revaluation of the hedged item. Illustration 4-3 below demonstrates how an entity treats the ineffectiveness not recognised on transition subsequently. Illustration 4-3: Ineffectiveness of fair value hedge on transition At the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item is CU100,000 whereas the cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument is CU80,000 (and therefore, the fair value adjustment to the hedged item is restricted to the change in fair value of the hedging instrument, i.e., CU80,000). Following the date from when the hedging relationship is designated under IFRS 9, subsequent changes in the fair value of the hedged item attributable to the hedged risk and changes in the fair value of the hedging instrument would be recognised in profit or loss (without reference to the adjustment made to the hedged item on transition. See also Case 1 in Illustration 4-5 in section 4.7 below for an example of adjustments required on transition for fair value hedges). [IFRS 9.6.5.8]. As time passes, the mechanics of fair value hedge accounting would normally result in an automatic unwinding of 100% of the cumulative fair value changes attributable to the hedged risk so that the carrying amount of the hedged item would, at any point in time, reflect fair value adjustments attributable to the hedged risk that are consistent with the remaining term of the hedging relationship. However, because at the date of transition, some of the cumulative changes in the fair value of the hedged item were not recognised, total fair value changes unwound, at any point in time, would outpace the cumulative adjustments applied to the hedged item up to that point. In this example, the hedged item carrying amount at maturity would differ from its nominal amount by CU20,000 since the original adjustment on transition was CU80,000 but the subsequent cumulative net change in fair value that would be recognised in profit or loss would be CU100,000. In order to deal with this difference, the CU20,000 would need to be amortised over the term of the hedge in profit or loss. For interest-bearing hedged items, this would typically be achieved using the effective interest rate method. [IFRS 9.6.5.10]. 4.6.4 ‘Costs of hedging’ (where excluded from the hedging relationship under previous GAAP) As explained in section 4.5.6 above, ‘costs of hedging’ that were amortised or deferred under previous GAAP, are recognised as a separate component of equity rather than retained earnings on transition, including any accumulated difference in the amount deferred under previous GAAP and the amount in accordance with IFRS accounting standards. IFRS 1 does not provide specific guidance on how to account for ‘costs of hedging’, including the difference arising on transition in subsequent periods. How we see it In our view, the same approach as that described earlier for cash flow hedges (see section 4.6.2 above) is applied to the ‘costs of hedging’. In cases where there is a transaction-related hedged item, any amount of ‘costs of hedging’ on transition deferred in equity will be: (a) included directly in the initial cost or other carrying amount of a non-financial asset or a non-financial liability on its initial recognition, or (b) reclassified to profit or loss as a reclassification adjustment in the same period or periods during which the hedged expected future cash flows affect profit or loss, depending on whether the hedged item results in a non-financial item or not. If there is a time-period related hedged item, the difference will be amortised on a systematic and rational basis over the remaining hedging periods. First-time adoption If the first-time adopter excludes ’costs of hedging’ from the designation of the hedging relationship prospectively under IFRS 9, and the entity is required (in the case of time value of options) or chooses (in the case of the forward element of forward contracts and foreign currency basis spreads) to apply the ‘costs of hedging’ guidance in IFRS 9, the ‘costs of hedging’ will subsequently be accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9.6.5.15 and IFRS 9.6.5.16 and IFRS 9.B6.5.29-B6.5.39. [IFRS 9.6.5.15, IFRS 9.6.5.16, IFRS 9.B6.5.29-B6.5.39]. If the first-time adopter chooses not to apply the ‘costs of hedging’ guidance to the forward element of forward contracts or foreign currency basis spread, movements in the fair value of the forward element or foreign currency basis spread are recognised in profit or loss. If the first-time adopter does not exclude the ‘costs of hedging’ from the designation of the hedging relationship prospectively under IFRS 9, normal hedge accounting is applied but this designation will give rise to ineffectiveness to be accounted for in profit or loss. Any amounts deferred in equity for ‘costs of hedging’ at the date of transition (see section 4.5.6 above) will be accounted for as described in the ‘How we see it’ above. See Chapter 49 at section 7.5 for further discussion of accounting for ‘cost of hedging’ under IFRS 9. 4.7 Hedge accounting: Illustrations Illustrations 4-4 and 4-5 below demonstrate the guidance considered in sections 4.5 to 4.6 above. Illustration 4-4: Pre-transition cash flow hedges Case 1: All hedge accounting qualifying criteria met from date of transition and thereafter In 20X0, Entity A borrowed CU10m from a bank. The terms of the loan provide that a floating rate coupon (based on a specified index) plus 2% is payable quarterly in arrears and the principal is repayable after 10 years. In 20X3, Entity A decided to ‘fix’ its coupon payments for the remainder of the term of the loan by entering into a seven-year pay-fixed, receive-floating interest rate swap. The swap has a notional amount of CU10m and the floating leg resets quarterly based on the same floating rate index as the loan. In Entity A’s final financial statements prepared under its previous GAAP, the swap was clearly identified as a hedging instrument in a hedge of the loan and was accounted for as such. The fair value of the swap was not recognised in Entity A’s statement of financial position and the periodic interest settlements were accrued and recognised as an adjustment to the loan interest expense. On 1 January 20X7, Entity A’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the loan and the swap were still in place and the swap had a positive fair value of CU1m and a CUnil carrying amount. In addition, Entity A met all the qualifying criteria in IFRS 9 to permit the use of hedge accounting for this arrangement throughout 20X7 and 20X8. In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity A would: • Recognise the interest rate swap as an asset at its fair value of CU1m • Credit CU1m to a separate component of equity, to be reclassified to profit or loss as the hedged transactions (future interest payments on the loan) affect profit or loss In addition, hedge accounting would be applied throughout 20X7 and 20X8. Case 2: Hedge terminated prior to date of transition The facts are as in Case 1 except that in April 20X6 Entity A decided to terminate the hedge and the interest rate swap was settled for its then positive fair value of CU1.5m. Under its previous GAAP, Entity A’s stated accounting policy in respect of terminated hedges was to defer any realised gain or loss on terminated hedging instruments where the hedged exposure remained. These gains or losses would be recognised in profit or loss at the same time as gains or losses on the hedged exposure. At the end of December 20X6, Entity A’s statement of financial position included a liability (unamortised gain) of CU1.4m. In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity A would: • Remove the deferred gain of CU1.4m from the statement of financial position • Credit CU1.4m to a separate component of equity, to be reclassified to profit or loss as the hedged transactions (future interest payments on the loan) affect profit or loss How we see it IFRS 1 does not explicitly address hedges terminated prior to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. In our view, because the terminated hedge relates to a transaction that has an ongoing risk exposure, the provisions of IFRS 9 on hedge discontinuance are applied to this relationship. g ## Contents - 1 January 20X2, the fair value of the inventory increased by CU47. In addition, Entity B met all the qualifying First-time adoption Illustration 4-5: Existing fair value hedges Case 1: All hedge accounting qualifying criteria met from date of transition and thereafter On 15 November 20X1, Entity B entered into a forward contract to sell 50,000 barrels of crude oil to hedge all changes in the fair value of certain inventory. Entity B prepares its first IFRS financial statements and its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. The historical cost of the forward contract is CUnil and at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards the forward had a negative fair value of CU50. In Entity B’s final financial statements prepared under its previous GAAP, the forward contract was clearly identified as a hedging instrument in a hedge of the inventory and was accounted for as such. The contract was recognised in the statement of financial position as a liability at its fair value and the resulting loss was deferred in the statement of financial position as an asset. In the period between 15 November 20X1 and criteria in IFRS 9 to permit the use of hedge accounting for this arrangement throughout 20X2 until the forward expired. In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity B would: • Continue to recognise the forward contract as a liability at its fair value of CU50 • Derecognise the CU50 deferred loss asset on the forward contract • Recognise the crude oil inventory at its historical cost plus CU47 (the lower of the change in fair value of the crude oil inventory, CU47, and that of the forward contract, CU50) • Record the net adjustment of CU3 in retained earnings In addition, hedge accounting would be applied throughout 20X2 until the forward contract expired. Case 2: All hedge accounting qualifying criteria met from date of transition and thereafter In 20X0 Entity C borrowed CU10m from a bank. The terms of the loan provide that a coupon of 8% is payable quarterly in arrears and the principal is repayable after 10 years. In 20X3, Entity C decided to alter its coupon payments for the remainder of the term of the loan by entering into a seven-year pay-floating, receive-fixed interest rate swap. The swap has a notional amount of CU10m and the floating leg resets quarterly based on a specified index. In Entity C’s final financial statements prepared under its previous GAAP, the swap was clearly identified as a hedging instrument in a hedge of the loan and accounted for as such. The fair value of the swap was not recognised in Entity C’s statement of financial position and the periodic interest settlements on the swap were accrued and recognised as an adjustment to the loan interest expense. On 1 January 20X7, Entity C’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the loan and the swap were still in place and the swap had a negative fair value of CU1m and a CUnil carrying amount. The cumulative change in the fair value of the loan attributable to changes in the floating interest rate was CU1.1m, although this change was not recognised in Entity C’s statement of financial position because the loan was accounted for at amortised cost. In addition, Entity C met all the qualifying criteria in IFRS 9 to permit the use of hedge accounting for this arrangement throughout 20X7 and 20X8. In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity C would: • Recognise the interest rate swap as a liability at its fair value of CU1m • Reduce the carrying amount of the loan by CU1m (the lower of the change in its fair value attributable to the hedged risk, CU1.1m, and that of the interest rate swap, CU1m) to CU9m In addition, hedge accounting would be applied throughout 20X7 and 20X8. Case 3: Hedge terminated prior to date of transition The facts are as in Case 2 above except that, in April 20X6, Entity C decided to terminate the fair value hedge and the interest rate swap was settled for its then negative fair value of CU1.5m. Under its previous GAAP, Entity C’s stated accounting policy in respect of terminated hedges was to defer any gain or loss on the hedging instrument as a liability or an asset where the hedged exposure remained and this gain or loss was recognised in profit or loss at the same time as the hedged exposure. At the end of December 20X6 the unamortised loss recognised as an asset in Entity C’s statement of financial position was CU1.4m. The cumulative change through April 20X6 in the fair value of the loan attributable to changes in the floating interest rate that had not been recognised was CU1.6m. In its opening IFRS statement of financial position, Entity C would: • Remove the deferred loss asset of CU1.4m from the statement of financial position • Reduce the carrying amount of the loan by CU1.4m (the lower of the change in its fair value attributable to the hedged risk, CU1.6m, and the change in value of the interest rate swap that was deferred in the statement of financial position, CU1.4m) The CU1.4m adjustment to the loan would be amortised to profit or loss over its remaining term. First-time adoption Case 4: Documentation completed after the date of transition The facts are as in Case 2 above except that, at the date of transition, Entity C had not prepared documentation that would allow it to apply hedge accounting under IFRS 9. Hedge documentation was subsequently prepared as a result of which the hedge qualified for hedge accounting with effect from the beginning of July 20X7 and through 20X8. As in Case 2, in its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity C would: • Recognise the interest rate swap as a liability at its fair value of CU1m • Reduce the carrying amount of the loan by CU1m (the lower of the change in its fair value attributable to the hedged risk, CU1.1m, and that of the interest rate swap, CU1m), because the loan was clearly identified as a hedged item For the period from January 20X7 to June 20X7, hedge accounting would not be available. Accordingly, the interest rate swap would be remeasured to its fair value and any gain or loss would be recognised in profit or loss with no offset from remeasuring the loan. The CU1m adjustment to the loan would be amortised to profit or loss over its remaining term (the amortisation could begin as soon as an adjustment exists and must begin no later than when the hedged item ceases to be adjusted for hedging gains and losses). With effect from July 20X7 hedge accounting would be applied prospectively. 4.8 Non-controlling interests A first-time adopter must apply IFRS 10 retrospectively, except for the following requirements that apply prospectively from its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards: [IFRS 1.B7] (a) The requirement that total comprehensive income is attributed to the owners of the parent and to the non-controlling interests even if this results in the non-controlling interests having a deficit balance [IFRS 10.B94] (b) The requirements on accounting for changes in the parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary that do not result in a loss of control [IFRS 10.23, IFRS 10.B96] (c) The requirements on accounting for a loss of control over a subsidiary, and the related requirements in IFRS 5 Non-current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations to classify all assets and liabilities of that subsidiary as held for sale [IFRS 10.B97, IFRS 10.B98, IFRS 10.B99, IFRS 5.8A] However, if a first-time adopter restates any business combination that occurred prior to its date of transition to comply with IFRS 3 Business Combinations it must also apply IFRS 10, including the above requirements, from that date onwards (see section 5.2.2 below). [IFRS 1.C1]. 4.9 Classification and measurement of financial instruments 4.9.1 Classification of financial assets IFRS 9 requires a financial asset to be measured at amortised cost if it meets two tests that deal with the nature of the business model that holds the financial asset and the contractual cash flow characteristics of the financial asset. [IFRS 9.4.1.2]. Also, the standard requires a financial asset to be measured at fair value through other comprehensive income if certain conditions are met. [IFRS 9.4.1.2A]. These conditions for classification of financial assets are described in detail in Chapter 44 at sections 1 and 1.1. IFRS 1.B8 requires a first-time adopter to assess whether a financial asset meets the conditions on the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.B8]. The resulting classifications are applied retrospectively. [IFRS 1.IG56]. 4.9.1.A Assessment of a modified time value of money element If it is impracticable to assess a modified time value of money element under IFRS 9.B4.1.9B-B4.1.9D on the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the first-time adopter is required to assess the contractual cash flow characteristics of that financial asset on the basis of the facts and circumstances that existed at that date of transition without taking into account the requirements related to the modification of the time value of money element in **IFRS 9.B4.1.9B-B4.1.9D.** In this case, the entity also applies IFRS 7.42R and discloses the carrying amount at the reporting date of the financial assets - whose contractual cash flow characteristics have been assessed based on the facts and circumstances that existed at the date of transition without taking into account the requirements related to the modification of the time value of money element in IFRS 9.B4.1.9B-B4.1.9D - until those financial assets are derecognised (see section 6.4.2 below). [IFRS 1.B8A, IFRS 7.42R, IFRS 9.B4.1.9B, IFRS 9.B4.1.9C, IFRS 9.B4.1.9D]. First-time adoption 4.9.1.B Assessment of whether the fair value of a prepayment feature is insignificant Similarly, if it is impracticable to assess whether the fair value of a prepayment feature is insignificant under IFRS 9.B4.1.12(c) on the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, IFRS 1.B8B requires an entity to assess the contractual cash flow characteristics of that financial asset on the basis of the facts and circumstances that existed at the date of transition without taking into account the exception for prepayment features in IFRS 9.B4.1.12 (see # Chapter 44 at section 5.4.4.B). In this case, the entity also applies IFRS 7.42S and discloses the carrying amount at the reporting date of the financial assets - whose contractual cash flow characteristics have been assessed based on the facts and circumstances that existed at the date of transition without taking into account the exception for prepayment features in IFRS 9.B4.1.12 - until those financial assets are derecognised (see section 6.4.2 below). [IFRS 1.B8B, IFRS 7.42S, IFRS 9.B4.1.12]. 4.9.2 Measurement of financial instruments measured at amortised cost For those financial assets and financial liabilities measured at amortised cost in the opening IFRS statement of financial position, an entity determines the gross carrying amount of the financial assets and the amortised cost of the financial liabilities on the basis of circumstances existing when the assets and liabilities first satisfied the recognition criteria in IFRS 9. However, if the entity acquired those financial assets and financial liabilities in a past business combination to which the entity elects not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively, their carrying amount in accordance with previous GAAP immediately following the business combination is their deemed cost in accordance with IFRS accounting standards at that date. [IFRS 1.C4(e), IFRS 1.IG57]. IFRS 1.B8C states that if it is impracticable (as defined in IAS 8) for an entity to apply retrospectively the effective interest method in IFRS 9, the fair value of the financial asset or the financial liability at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards will be the new gross carrying amount of that financial asset or the new amortised cost of that financial liability at the date of transition. [IFRS 1.B8C, IAS 8.5]. 4.9.3 Transition adjustments An entity treats an adjustment to the carrying amount of a financial asset or financial liability as a transition adjustment to be recognised in the opening balance of retained earnings (or another component of equity, as appropriate) at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards only to the extent that it results from adopting IFRS 9. [IFRS 1.IG58A]. If an entity is unable to determine whether a particular portion of the adjustment is a transition adjustment or a change in estimate, it treats that portion as a change in accounting estimate in accordance with IAS 8, with appropriate disclosures, [IFRS 1.IG58B], (see section 4.2 above). If an investment had been measured at fair value under previous GAAP with revaluation gains recognised outside profit or loss but the investment is classified as at fair value through profit or loss under IFRS 9, the pre-IFRS 9 revaluation gain that had been recognised outside profit or loss is reclassified into retained earnings on initial application of IFRS 9. If, on initial application of IFRS 9, the investment is measured at fair value through other comprehensive income in accordance with IFRS 9.4.1.2A or is designated at fair value through other comprehensive income in accordance with IFRS 9.5.7.5, then the pre-IFRS 9 revaluation gain is recognised in a separate component of equity. Subsequently, the entity recognises gains and losses on these financial assets in accordance with IFRS 9. [IFRS 1.IG59, IFRS 9.4.1.2A, IFRS 9.5.7.5]. ### Impairment of financial assets IFRS 1.B8D requires a first-time adopter to apply the impairment requirements in Section 5.5 of IFRS 9 retrospectively subject to IFRS 1.B8E-B8G. [IFRS 1.B8D, IFRS 1.B8E, IFRS 1.B8F, IFRS 1.B8G]. At the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, IFRS 1.B8E requires a first-time adopter to use reasonable and supportable information that is available without undue cost or effort (see Chapter 47 at section 5.9) to determine the credit risk at the date that the financial instruments were initially recognised (or, for loan commitments and financial guarantee contracts, the date that the entity became a party to the irrevocable commitment in accordance with IFRS 9.5.5.6) and compare that to the credit risk at the date of transition. IFRS 1.B8E references IFRS 9.B7.2.2-B7.2.3 which set out similar transitional rules on adoption of IFRS 9 for existing IFRS reporters. [IFRS 1.B8E, IFRS 9.5.5.6, IFRS 9.B7.2.2, IFRS 9.B7.2.3]. First-time adoption In order to determine the loss allowance on financial instruments initially recognised (or loan commitments or financial guarantee contracts to which the entity became a party to the contract) prior to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, both on the transition and until the derecognition of those items, an entity is required to consider information that is relevant in determining or approximating the credit risk at initial recognition. In order to determine or approximate the initial credit risk, an entity could consider internal and external information, including portfolio information, in accordance with IFRS 9.B5.5.1-B5.5.6 (see Chapter 47 at section 6). [IFRS 9.B5.5.1-B5.5.6, IFRS 9.B7.2.3]. A first-time adopter is allowed to apply the following guidance to determine whether there has been a significant increase in credit risk since initial recognition: [IFRS 1.B8F] (a) The requirements (on financial instruments determined to have low credit risk) in IFRS 9.5.5.10 and IFRS 9.B5.5.22-B5.5.24 (see Chapter 47 at section 6.4.1) [IFRS 9.5.5.10, IFRS 9.B5.5.22, IFRS 9.B5.5.23, **IFRS 9.B5.5.24]** (b) The rebuttable presumption in IFRS 9.5.5.11 (that there has been a significant increase in credit risk since initial recognition) for contractual payments that are more than 30 days past due if an entity will apply the impairment requirements by identifying significant increases in credit risk since initial recognition for those financial instruments on the basis of past due information (see Chapter 47 at section 6.2.2) [IFRS 9.5.5.11] A first-time adopter would not be required to determine whether there has been a significant increase in credit risk if that would require undue cost or effort. In such a case, IFRS 1.B8G requires a first-time adopter to recognise a loss allowance at an amount equal to lifetime expected credit losses at each reporting date until that financial instrument is derecognised (unless that financial asset is low credit risk at a reporting date, in which case, IFRS 1.B8F(a) applies and, consequently, the entity is allowed to assume that the credit risk has not increased significantly since initial recognition (see Chapter 47 at section 6.4.1)). [IFRS 1.B8F(a), IFRS 1.B8G, IFRS 9.5.5.10, IFRS 9.B5.5.22-B5.5.24]. ### Embedded derivatives When IFRS 9 requires an entity to separate an embedded derivative from a host contract, [IFRS 9.4.3.3], the initial carrying amounts of the components at the date the instrument first satisfied the recognition criteria in IFRS 9 must reflect circumstances that existed at that date. If the initial carrying amounts of the embedded derivative and host contract cannot be determined reliably, the entire combined contract is designated at fair value through profit or loss. [IFRS 1.IG55, IFRS 9.4.3.6]. IFRS 1.B9 requires a first-time adopter to assess whether an embedded derivative is required to be separated from the host contract and accounted for as a derivative based on conditions that existed at the later of the date it first became a party to the contract and the date a reassessment is required by IFRS 9.B4.3.11. [IFRS 1.B9, IFRS 9.B4.3.11]. It should be noted that IFRS 9 does not permit embedded derivatives to be separated from host contracts that are financial assets (see Chapter 42 at section 3). ### Government loans It is common practice in certain countries for the government to grant loans to entities at below-market interest rates, for example, in order to promote economic development. A first-time adopter might not have recognised and measured such loans in its previous GAAP financial statements on a basis that complies with IFRS accounting standards. IAS 20 Accounting for Government Grants and Disclosure of Government Assistance requires such loans to be recognised initially at fair value with the effect of the below-market interest rate separately accounted for as a government grant (see Chapter 25 at section 3.4). [IAS 20.10A]. The IASB has provided transition relief to first-time adopters in the form of an exception that requires the first-time adopter to classify government loans received as a financial liability or an equity instrument in accordance with IAS 32 Financial Instruments: Presentation and to apply the requirements in IFRS 9 and IAS 20 prospectively to government loans existing at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (except as permitted by IFRS 1.B11, which is discussed below). Therefore, a first-time adopter does not recognise the corresponding benefit of the government loan at a below-market rate of interest as a government grant. A first-time adopter that did not, under its previous GAAP, recognise and measure a government loan at a below-market rate of interest on a basis consistent with IFRS accounting standards requirements will instead use its previous GAAP carrying amount of the loan at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards as the carrying amount of the loan in the opening IFRS statement of financial position and apply IFRS 9 to the measurement of such loans after the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.B10]. First-time adoption Alternatively, IFRS 1.B11 allows an entity to apply the requirements in IFRS 9 and IAS 20 retrospectively to any government loan originated before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, provided the information needed to do so had been obtained when it initially accounted for the loan under previous **GAAP. [IFRS 1.B11].** The requirements and guidance set out in IFRS 1.B10-B11 (which are discussed above) do not preclude an entity from using the exemptions in IFRS 1.D19-D19C to designate previously recognised financial instruments at fair value through profit or loss (see section 5.10 below). [IFRS 1.B12, IFRS 1.D19, IFRS 1.D19A, **IFRS 1.D19B, IFRS 1.D19C].** Illustration 4-6 below demonstrates the accounting for a government loan with a below-market interest rate that is not restated from its previous GAAP amount. [IFRS 1.IG Example 12, IFRS 1.IG66]. Illustration 4-6: Government loan with below-market interest rate A government provides loans at a below-market rate of interest to fund the purchase of manufacturing equipment. Entity A’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. In 20X0, Entity A received a loan of CU100,000 at a below-market rate of interest from the government. Under its previous GAAP, Entity A accounted for the loan as equity and the carrying amount was CU100,000 at the date of transition. The amount repayable at 1 January 20X5 will be CU103,030. No other payment is required under the terms of the loan and there are no future performance conditions attached to it. The information needed to measure the fair value of the loan was not obtained at the time it was initially accounted for. The loan meets the definition of a financial liability in accordance with IAS 32. Entity A therefore reclassifies it from equity to liability. It also uses the previous GAAP carrying amount of the loan at the date of transition as the carrying amount of the loan in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. It calculates the effective interest rate starting at 1 January 20X2 as 1% based on the carrying amount of the loan at the date of transition and the amount repayable at maturity. The opening balance of CU100,000 will accrete to CU103,030 at 31 December 20X4 and interest of CU1,000, CU1,010 and CU1,020 will be charged to interest expense in each of the three years ended 31 December 20X2, 20X3 and 20X4. ### Insurance contracts An entity must apply the transition provisions in IFRS 17.C1-C24 and IFRS 17.C28 to contracts within the scope of IFRS 17 Insurance Contracts (see Chapter 51 at section 17). The references in those paragraphs in IFRS 17 to ‘the transition date’ must be read as ‘the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards’. [IFRS 1.B13]. This is because the Board saw no reason to give different transition approaches to first-time adopters of IFRS accounting standards from other entities. This means that the Board has amended IFRS 1 to require use of the modified retrospective approach or the fair value approach in IFRS 17 when retrospective application of IFRS 17 is impracticable, as defined by IAS 8. However, the Board decided not to give any additional relief on the restatement of comparative amounts from that already in IFRS 1. [IFRS 17.BC407]. ### Deferred tax related to leases and decommissioning, restoration and similar liabilities IAS 12 Income Taxes provides an exception (known as the ‘initial recognition exception’) from recognising the deferred tax on temporary differences arising on initial recognition of an asset or liability in a transaction that (a) is not a business combination; (b) at the time of the transaction, affects neither accounting profit nor taxable profit (or tax loss); and (c) at the time of the transaction does not give rise to equal taxable and deductible temporary differences (see Chapter 29 at section 7.3). [IAS 12.15, IAS 12.24]. For example, at the commencement date of a lease, a lessee typically recognises a lease liability and the corresponding amount as part of the cost of a right-of-use asset. Depending on the applicable tax law, equal taxable and deductible temporary differences could arise on initial recognition of the asset and liability in such a transaction. If so, the initial recognition exception does not apply and any resulting deferred tax liability and asset is recognised. [IAS 12.22A]. It is important to note that IAS 12.15(b)(iii) and IAS 12.24(c) refer to ‘equal taxable and deductible temporary differences’, rather than ‘equal deferred tax assets and liabilities’. For example, the deferred tax assets and liabilities recognised could differ due to the application of different tax rates or because the deferred tax assets are not recoverable. [IAS 12.BC86, IAS 12.BC88]. First-time adoption Retrospective application would require entities to retrospectively assess whether each lease and decommissioning obligation gave rise to equal taxable and deductible temporary differences on initial recognition, which might have occurred many years before. Instead, first-time adopters are required to recognise deferred tax for all temporary differences associated with leases and decommissioning obligations existing at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IAS 12.BC95]. Therefore, notwithstanding the initial recognition exception in IAS 12.15 and IAS 12.24, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, a first-time adopter must recognise a deferred tax asset (to the extent that it is probable that taxable profit will be available against which the deductible temporary difference can be utilised) and a deferred tax liability for all deductible and taxable temporary differences associated with: [IFRS 1.B14]. (a) Right-of-use assets and lease liabilities (b) Decommissioning, restoration and similar liabilities and the corresponding amounts recognised as part of the cost of the related asset However, the initial recognition exception still applies fully retrospectively to transactions other than leases and decommissioning obligations. First-time adoption Optional exemptions from the requirements of certain IFRS accounting standards 5.1 Introduction As noted in section 3.5 above, IFRS 1 grants limited optional exemptions from the general requirement of full retrospective application of the standards effective at the end of an entity’s first IFRS reporting period. [IFRS 1.7, IFRS 1.12(b)]. Each of these exemptions, which are set out in Appendices C and D of IFRS 1, is explained in detail below. 5.2 Business combinations and acquisitions of associates and joint arrangements The business combinations exemption in IFRS 1 is probably the single most important exemption in the standard, as it permits a first-time adopter not to restate business combinations that occurred prior to its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The detailed guidance on the application of the business combinations exemption is contained in Appendix C to IFRS 1 and is organised in the sections as follows: [IFRS 1 Appendix C] (a) Scope of the business combinations exemption in IFRS 1 - and application of the exemption to acquisitions of associates and joint arrangements (see section 5.2.1 below) (b) Option to restate business combinations retrospectively (see section 5.2.2 below) (c) Classification of business combinations (see section 5.2.3 below) (d) Assets and liabilities to be recognised in the opening IFRS statement of financial position (see section 5.2.4 below) (e) Restatement of goodwill (see section 5.2.5 below) (f) Currency adjustments to goodwill (see section 5.2.6 below) (g) Previously unconsolidated subsidiaries (see section 5.2.7 below) (h) Previously consolidated entities that are not subsidiaries (see section 5.2.8 below) (i) Measurement of deferred taxes and non-controlling interests (see section 5.2.9 below) (j) Transition accounting for contingent consideration (see section 5.2.10 below) How we see it In our view, the business combinations exemption applies only to business combinations that occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and only to business combinations as defined under IFRS 3 (see section 5.2.1 below). Therefore, it does not apply to a transaction that, for example, IFRS accounting standards treat as an acquisition of an asset (see section 5.2.1.A below). 5.2.1 Scope of the business combinations exemption in IFRS 1 As noted above, the business combinations exemption applies only to business combinations as defined under IFRS 3. Therefore, a first-time adopter needs to consider whether past transactions would qualify as business combinations under IFRS 3 which defines a business combination as: “A transaction or other event in which an acquirer obtains control of one or more businesses. Transactions sometimes referred to as ‘true mergers’ or ‘mergers of equals’ are also business combinations as that term is used in this IFRS.” [IFRS 3 Appendix A]. The definition of a business is “An integrated set of activities and assets that is capable of being conducted and managed for the purpose of providing goods or services to customers, generating investment income (such as dividends or interest) or generating other income from ordinary activities.” [IFRS 3 Appendix A]. In addition, IFRS 3.3 states that “If the assets acquired are not a business, the reporting entity shall account for the transaction or other event as an asset acquisition.” (See section 5.2.1.A below). [IFRS 3.3]. Distinguishing a business combination from an asset acquisition is described in detail in Chapter 10 at section 3. First-time adoption In October 2012, the IASB issued Investment Entities: Amendments to IFRS 10, IFRS 12 and IAS 27 (see section 5.8.5 below). Amongst other changes, the amendment also stated that Appendix C of IFRS 1, which deals with the business combinations exemption, is applied only to business combinations within the scope of IFRS 3. [IFRS 1 Appendix C, IFRS 3.2A]. How we see it In our view, the purpose of this change was to preclude the application of Appendix C to the acquisition by an investment entity, as defined in IFRS 10, of an investment in subsidiary that is required to be measured at fair value through profit or loss. Therefore, we believe that the exemption is still available to transactions that meet the definition of a business combination under IFRS 3, even if the business combination is outside the scope of IFRS 3 (such as a combination of entities or businesses under common control). However, the business combinations exemption does not apply to transactions which are not ‘business combinations’ as defined in IFRS 3 (even if the transactions were accounted for as business combinations under previous GAAP). Examples of transactions that are not business combinations would include asset acquisitions (see section 5.2.1.A below) or continuations (such as where a new holding company is inserted above an existing group). A first-time adopter which had such transactions under previous GAAP retrospectively applies IFRS accounting standards applicable to those transactions. If transactions which are not business combinations as defined in IFRS 3 are combinations of entities under common control, there is no specific guidance under IFRS accounting standards on accounting for them. We discuss this issue in Chapter 11 at sections 1.2, 1.3, 2 and 4. 5.2.1.A Asset acquisitions Because IFRS 3 provides such a specific definition of a business combination (as described in Chapter 10 at section 3), it is possible that under some national GAAPs, transactions that are not business combinations under IFRS 3, e.g., asset acquisitions, could have been accounted for as if they were business combinations. If so, a first-time adopter will need to restate such transactions, as discussed in Illustration 5-1 below. Illustration 5-1: Acquisition of assets Entity A acquired a company that held a single asset at the time of acquisition. That company had no employees and the asset itself was not in use at the date of acquisition. Entity A accounted for the transaction under its previous GAAP using the purchase method, which resulted in goodwill. Can Entity A apply the business combinations exemption to the acquisition of this asset? If Entity A concludes that the asset is not a business as defined in IFRS 3, it will not be able to apply the business combinations exemption. Instead, Entity A accounts for such transaction as an asset acquisition (see Chapter 10 at section 2.2.2). Entity A could consider applying other applicable optional exemptions under IFRS 1. How we see it If, in Illustration 5-1 above, the entity had accounted for the transaction as an asset acquisition rather than a business combination under previous GAAP, it would need to determine whether the transaction meets the definition of a business combination in IFRS 3 in order to qualify for use of the exemption. 5.2.1.B Associates and joint arrangements Extract from IFRS 1 C5 The exemption for past business combinations also applies to past acquisitions of investments in associates, interests in joint ventures and interests in joint operations in which the activity of the joint operation constitutes a business, as defined in IFRS 3. Furthermore, the date selected for paragraph C1 applies equally for all such acquisitions. IFRS 1 extends the exemption for past business combinations to past acquisitions of investments in associates, interests in joint ventures and interests in joint operations (in which the activity of the joint operation constitutes a business, as defined in IFRS 3). The date selected for the first restatement of business combinations will also be applied to the restatement of these other acquisitions. [IFRS 1.C5]. First-time adoption 5.2.2 Option to restate business combinations retrospectively Extract from IFRS 1 C1 A first-time adopter may elect not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to past business combinations (business combinations that occurred before the date of transition to IFRSs). However, if a first-time adopter restates any business combination to comply with IFRS 3, it shall restate all later business combinations and shall also apply IFRS 10 from that same date. For example, if a first-time adopter elects to restate a business combination that occurred on ## June 20X6, it shall restate all business combinations that occurred between 30 June 20X6 and the date of transition to IFRSs, and it shall also apply IFRS 10 from 30 June 20X6. A first-time adopter must account for business combinations occurring after its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards under IFRS 3, i.e., any business combinations during the comparative periods presented need to be restated in accordance with IFRS 3. An entity accounts for a business combination under common control based on its accounting policy as determined under IFRS accounting standards (see # Chapter 11 at section 3). This is because such transactions are outside the scope of IFRS 3. [IFRS 3.2(c)]. A first-time adopter is allowed to elect not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to business combinations occurring before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. However, if a first-time adopter does restate a business combination occurring prior to its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards to comply with IFRS 3 it must also restate any subsequent business combinations under IFRS 3 and apply IFRS 10 from that date onwards. [IFRS 1.C1]. In other words, as shown on the timeline at Figure 5-1 below, a first-time adopter is allowed to choose any date in the past from which it wants to account for all business combinations under IFRS 3 and would not restate business combinations that occurred prior to such date. It must be noted that a first-time adopter availing itself of this option is required to apply the version of IFRS 3 effective at the end of its first IFRS reporting period to any retrospectively restated business combinations. [IFRS 1.7]. Appendix C to IFRS 1 sets out the accounting consequences for business combinations not restated in accordance with IFRS 3 (see sections 5.2.3 to 5.2.10 below). [IFRS 1.C2, IFRS 1.C3, IFRS 1.C4]. However, the exemptions set out in IFRS 1.C2-C3 from applying IAS 21 to goodwill and fair value adjustments are also available for business combinations that are restated in accordance with IFRS 3. [IFRS 1.C2, IFRS 1.C3]. The various options for applying IAS 21 to goodwill and fair value adjustments are explained further in section 5.2.6 below. If any pre-transition business combinations are restated, the first-time adopter must also apply IFRS 10’s requirements on: the attribution of total comprehensive income to the owners of the parent and to the non-controlling interests; accounting for changes in the parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary without loss of control; and accounting for a loss of control of a subsidiary (and the related IFRS 5 requirements on classification of the disposal group as ‘held for sale’) from the date of the earliest business combination that is restated (see section 4.8 above). [IFRS 1.B7, IFRS 1.C1]. Figure 5-1: Application of the optional business combination exemption Even if a first-time adopter elects not to restate certain business combinations, it might still need to restate the carrying amounts of the acquired assets and assumed liabilities, as described in section 5.2.4 below. How we see it Although there is no restriction that prevents retrospective application by a first-time adopter of IFRS 3 to all past business combinations, in our view, a first-time adopter does not restate business combinations under IFRS 3 that occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards when this would require use of hindsight. First-time adoption Practical example 5-1 below and Practical example 6-1 in section 6.3 below are examples of disclosures made by entities that opted not to restate business combinations that occurred before their date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, while Practical example 5-2 below illustrates disclosures by an entity that chose to restate certain business combinations that occurred prior to its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. Practical example 5-1: Husky Energy Inc. (2011) Canada Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] NOTES TO THE CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] Note 26 First-Time Adoption of International Financial Reporting Standards [extract] … Notes to the Reconciliations of Equity and Comprehensive Income from Canadian GAAP to IFRS [extract] First-time adoption Practical example 5-2: The Toronto-Dominion Bank (2011) Canada ## CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS AND NOTES [extract] Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] NOTE 34 TRANSITION TO IFRS [extract] **DESCRIPTION OF SIGNIFICANT MEASUREMENT AND PRESENTATION DIFFERENCES BETWEEN** CANADIAN GAAP AND IFRS [extract] 5.2.3 Classification of business combinations IFRS 3 mandates a business combination to be accounted for either as an acquisition or reverse acquisition. [IFRS 3.4, IFRS 3.5(a), IFRS 3.6, IFRS 3.B19]. An entity’s previous GAAP could be based on a different definition of, for example, a business combination, an acquisition, a uniting of interests (or merger) or a reverse acquisition. An important benefit of the exemption not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to pre-transition business combinations is that a first-time adopter keeps the same classification (as in its previous GAAP financial statements) for business combinations not restated under IFRS 3, [IFRS 1.C4(a)]. For example, a business combination that was accounted for as a merger (or uniting of interests) using the pooling of interests method under an entity’s previous GAAP is not reclassified and accounted for under the acquisition method. Similarly, a business combination that was accounted for as an acquisition by the legal acquirer under the entity’s previous GAAP continues to be classified as such even if it would be accounted for as a reverse acquisition by the acquiree under IFRS 3.
