Neither IFRS 1 nor IFRS 2 clearly indicates the appropriate treatment of the costs of share-based payment transactions that were recognised under the previous GAAP. How we see it There are mixed views on this issue in different jurisdictions, some of which are being driven by local regulatory expectations. In practice, either of the following approaches is considered acceptable, provided that the treatment chosen is disclosed in the financial statements if the previously recognised costs are material: • Not to change expense previously recognised under previous GAAP for equity instruments subject to transitional relief under IFRS 1. This approach is consistent with the approach for cash-settled share-based payment transactions that are settled prior to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, since the entity must reflect the reduction in cash that was actually paid (and therefore the reduction in retained earnings) in the first IFRS financial statements. Or • To derecognise share-based payment expense under previous GAAP for equity instruments subject to transitional relief under IFRS 1. In other words, reverse cumulative amounts recognised in the retained earnings and equity accounts of the opening IFRS statement of financial position. First-time adoption 5.4 Deemed cost IFRS 1 requires full retrospective application of IFRS accounting standards effective at the end of a first- time adopter’s first IFRS reporting period. [IFRS 1.7]. Therefore, in the absence of the deemed cost exemption, the requirements of, for example, IAS 16, IAS 38, IAS 40 Investment Property and IFRS 6 Exploration for and Evaluation of Mineral Resources would have to be applied as if the first-time adopter had always applied these standards. This could be quite onerous because: • These items are long-lived which means that accounting records from the time of acquisition might not be available anymore. In the case of formerly state-owned businesses, the required accounting records possibly never even existed • The entity might have revalued the items in the past as a matter of accounting policy or because this was required under national law Or • Even if the items were carried at depreciated cost, the accounting policy for recognition and depreciation might not have been compliant with IFRS accounting standards Given the significance of items like property, plant and equipment in the statement of financial position of most first-time adopters and the sheer number of transactions affecting property, plant and equipment, restatement is not only difficult but would often also involve huge cost and effort. Therefore, the IASB decided to introduce the notion of a ‘deemed cost’ that is not the ‘true’ cost basis of an asset (as required by IFRS accounting standards), but a surrogate that is deemed to be a suitable starting point. There are seven separate deemed cost exemptions in IFRS 1: (a) Fair value or revaluation as deemed cost (see section 5.4.1 below) (b) Event-driven fair value measurement as deemed cost (see section 5.4.2 below) (c) Deemed cost for oil and gas assets (see section 5.4.3 below) (d) Deemed cost for assets used (or previously used) in operations subject to rate regulation (see section 5.4.4 below) (e) Deemed cost for assets acquired, liabilities assumed and goodwill in a business combination not restated under IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.2 above) (f) Deemed cost for a right-of-use asset for a lessee based on certain exemptions in IFRS 1 (see section 5.5 below) (g) Deemed cost in determining the cost of an investment in a subsidiary, joint venture or associate in separate financial statements (see section 5.7.2 below) 5.4.1 Fair value or revaluation as deemed cost To deal with the problem of restatement of long-lived assets upon first-time adoption of IFRS accounting standards, IFRS 1 permits a first-time adopter - for the categories of assets listed below - to measure an item in its opening IFRS statement of financial position using an amount that is based on its deemed cost (see section 5.4.1.A below): [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6, IFRS 1.D7] • Property, plant and equipment, including bearer plants (see section 7.4 below) [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6] • Investment property, if an entity elects to use the cost model in IAS 40. [IFRS 1.D7(a)]. The fact that the exemption can only be applied to investment property accounted for under the cost model will not pose any problems in practice as the fair value model under IAS 40 requires an entity to measure its investment property at fair value at its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. • Right-of-use assets under IFRS 16 [IFRS 1.D7(aa)] • Intangible assets (see section 7.14 below) that meet: [IFRS 1.D7(b)] • The recognition criteria in IAS 38 (including reliable measurement of original cost) And • The criteria in IAS 38 for revaluation (including the existence of an active market) A first-time adopter cannot use this deemed cost approach by analogy for any other assets or for liabilities. **[IFRS 1.D7].** The use of fair value or revaluation as deemed cost for intangible assets will be very limited in practice because of the definition of an ‘active market’ in IFRS 13. An active market is defined as: “A market in which transactions for the asset or liability take place with sufficient frequency and volume to provide pricing information on an ongoing basis.” [IFRS 13 Appendix A]. It is therefore unlikely that a first-time adopter will be able to apply this exemption to any intangible assets (see Chapter 18 at section 8.2). First-time adoption It is important to note that this exemption does not take classes or categories of assets as its unit of measure, but refers to “an item of property, plant and equipment”, and similarly for investment property, right-of-use assets under IFRS 16 and intangible assets. [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6, IFRS 1.D7]. How we see it A first-time adopter is allowed to apply the deemed cost exemption to only some of its assets. For example, it could apply the exemption only to: • A selection of properties • Part of a factory Or • Some of the right-of-use asset leased under a single lease agreement This is because IFRS 1.IG12 states that “IAS 16 does not prescribe the unit of measure for recognition of an asset, i.e., what constitutes an item of property, plant and equipment. Thus, judgement is required in applying the recognition criteria to an entity’s specific circumstances” (see Chapter 19 at section 3.1). The IASB argued that it is not necessary to restrict application of the exemption to classes of assets to prevent arbitrarily selective revaluations, because IAS 36 “requires an impairment test if there is any indication that an asset is impaired. Thus, if an entity uses fair value as deemed cost for assets whose fair value is above cost, it cannot ignore indications that the recoverable amount of other assets may have fallen below their carrying amount. Therefore, the IFRS does not restrict the use of fair value as deemed cost to entire classes of asset.” [IFRS 1.BC45]. How we see it In our view, it is doubtful that the quality of financial information would benefit from a revaluation of a haphazard selection of items of property, plant and equipment as deemed cost. Therefore, a first-time adopter needs to exercise judgement in selecting the items to which it believes it is appropriate to apply the exemption. Practical examples 5-4 and 5-5 below refer to the use of the ‘fair value or revaluation as deemed cost’ exemption. Practical example 5-4: Suncor Energy Inc. (2011) Canada ## ANNUAL REPORT [extract] ## Contents - 6. FIRST-TIME ADOPTION OF IFRS [extract] NOTES TO THE CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] Explanation of Significant Adjustments [extract] First-time adoption Practical example 5-5: Nexen Inc. (2011) Canada ## ANNUAL REPORT [extract] - 26. TRANSITION TO IFRS [extract] NOTES TO CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] … 5.4.1.A Determining deemed cost IFRS 1 sets out the following options for deemed cost of an item of property, plant and equipment. [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6]. As noted in section 5.4.1 above, the same options are available for certain other assets. [IFRS 1.D7]. Extract from IFRS 1 D5 An entity may elect to measure an item of property, plant and equipment at the date of transition to IFRSs at its fair value and use that fair value as its deemed cost at that date. D6 A first-time adopter may elect to use a previous GAAP revaluation of an item of property, plant and equipment at, or before, the date of transition to IFRSs as deemed cost at the date of the revaluation, if the revaluation was, at the date of the revaluation, broadly comparable to: (a) fair value; or (b) cost or depreciated cost in accordance with IFRSs, adjusted to reflect, for example, changes in a general or specific price index. The ‘previous GAAP revaluations’ referred to in IFRS 1.D6 need only be “broadly comparable to fair value or reflect an index applied to a cost that is broadly comparable to cost determined in accordance with IFRSs”. [IFRS 1.BC47, IFRS 1.D6]. It appears that in the interest of practicality the IASB is allowing a good deal of flexibility in this matter. The IASB explains in the Basis for Conclusions that “It may not always be clear whether a previous revaluation was intended as a measure of fair value or differs materially from fair value. The flexibility in this area permits a cost-effective solution for the unique problem of transition to IFRSs. It allows a first-time adopter to establish a deemed cost using a measurement that is already available and is a reasonable starting point for a cost-based measurement.” [IFRS 1.BC47]. How we see it IFRS 1 describes the deemed cost in IFRS 1.D6 as a ‘previous GAAP revaluation’. Therefore, in our view, a revaluation can be used as the basis for deemed cost only if it was recognised in the first-time adopter’s previous GAAP financial statements. In some circumstances, as discussed below, a previous GAAP impairment (or its reversal) could represent a ‘previous GAAP revaluation’. A previous GAAP impairment (or reversal of an impairment) that resulted in the recognition of the related assets at fair value in the previous GAAP financial statements is allowed to be recognised as a previous GAAP revaluation for the purposes of applying this exemption only if it is broadly comparable to the fair value under IFRS 13 or cost (or depreciated cost) in accordance with IFRS accounting standards, adjusted to reflect, for example, changes in a general or specific price index and not another measure such as fair value less costs of disposal. First-time adoption Moreover, when the previous GAAP impairment was determined for a group of impaired assets (e.g., a cash generating unit as defined in IAS 36, see Chapter 21 at section 3), the recognised value of an individual asset needs to be broadly comparable to the specific asset’s fair value under IFRS 13 or cost (or depreciated cost) in accordance with IFRS accounting standards, adjusted to reflect, for example, changes in a general or specific price index and not another measure such as fair value less costs of disposal or an allocation thereof for the purposes of this exemption. [IFRS 1.D6]. If revaluations under previous GAAP did not satisfy the criteria in IFRS 1.D6 (as discussed above) or IFRS 1.D8 (see section 5.4.2 below), a first-time adopter measures the revalued assets within the scope of IAS 16 in its opening IFRS statement of financial position on one of the following bases: [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6, **IFRS 1.D8, IFRS 1.IG11]** (a) Cost (or deemed cost) less any accumulated depreciation and any accumulated impairment losses under the cost model in IAS 16 (b) Deemed cost, being the fair value at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards Or (c) Revalued amount, if the entity adopts the revaluation model in IAS 16 as its accounting policy under IFRS accounting standards for all items of property, plant and equipment in the same class The elections in IFRS 1.D6 or IFRS 1.D8 are also available for use for investment property (measured under the cost model), right-of-use assets, and intangible assets meeting certain criteria (see section 5.4.1 above). [IFRS 1.D7]. If the elections in IFRS 1.D6 or IFRS 1.D8 are not permitted for such assets, a first-time adopter measures the asset in its opening IFRS statement of financial position on one of the bases above, i.e., applying the cost model, deemed cost or a revalued amount in accordance with relevant IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6, IFRS 1.D7, IFRS 1.D8]. A right-of-use asset recognised by a lessee under IFRS 16 could also be measured at a carrying amount based on certain exemptions in IFRS 1 (see section 5.5 below). A first-time adopter that uses fair value as deemed cost is required to disclose the resulting IFRS 1 adjustment separately (see section 6.5.1 below). [IFRS 1.30]. 5.4.1.B Deemed cost determined before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards If the deemed cost of an asset was determined before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, then an IFRS accounting policy needs to be applied to that deemed cost in the intervening period to determine what the carrying amount of the asset is in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. This means that a first-time adopter that uses previous GAAP revaluation as the deemed cost of an item of property, plant and equipment will need to start depreciating the item from the date when the entity established the previous GAAP revaluation and not from its date of transition. [IFRS 1.IG9]. Illustration 5-20 below demonstrates the application of this requirement. Illustration 5-20: Deemed cost of property, plant and equipment Entity A used to revalue items of property, plant and equipment to fair value under its previous GAAP, but changed its accounting policy on 1 January 20X0 when it adopted a different accounting policy. Under that accounting policy, Entity A did not depreciate the asset and only recognised the maintenance costs as an expense. Entity A’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. In its balance sheet under previous GAAP the carrying amount of the asset is CU80,000 at the date of transition, which is equal to the last revaluation. Entity A can use the last revalued amount as the deemed cost of the asset on 1 January 20X0. [IFRS 1.D6]. However, Entity A will need to apply IAS 16 to the period after ## January 20X0 because the accounting policy under its previous GAAP is not permitted under IFRS accounting standards. Assuming that the economic life of the asset is 40 years from 20X0 and that the residual value is nil, Entity A would account for the asset at CU76,000 in its opening IFRS statement of financial position, which represents the deemed cost minus 2 years of depreciation. [IFRS 1.IG9]. 5.4.2 Event-driven fair value measurement as deemed cost A first-time adopter is allowed to use fair value measurements that arose from an event such as a privatisation or initial public offering as deemed cost for IFRS accounting standards at the date of that measurement. [IFRS 1.D8]. First-time adoption Extract from IFRS 1 D8 A first-time adopter may have established a deemed cost in accordance with previous GAAP for some or all of its assets and liabilities by measuring them at their fair value at one particular date because of an event such as a privatisation or initial public offering. (a) If the measurement date is at or before the date of transition to IFRSs, the entity may use such event-driven fair value measurements as deemed cost for IFRSs at the date of that measurement. (b) If the measurement date is after the date of transition to IFRSs, but during the period covered by the first IFRS financial statements, the event-driven fair value measurements may be used as deemed cost when the event occurs. An entity shall recognise the resulting adjustments directly in retained earnings (or if appropriate, another category of equity) at the measurement date. At the date of transition to IFRSs, the entity shall either establish the deemed cost by applying the criteria in paragraphs D5-D7 or measure assets and liabilities in accordance with the other requirements in this IFRS. IFRS 1 describes these revaluations as ‘deemed cost in accordance with previous GAAP’. Therefore, to the extent that they related to an event that occurred prior to its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, they can be used as the basis for deemed cost only if they were recognised in the first-time adopter’s previous GAAP financial statements. As discussed in section 5.4.2.C below, a first-time adopter is also allowed to use event-driven fair values resulting from such events that occurred subsequent to the first-time adopter’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, but during the period covered by the first IFRS financial statements. How we see it The ‘fair value or revaluation as deemed cost’ exemption discussed in section 5.4.1 above only applies to items of property, plant and equipment, investment property, right-of-use assets and certain intangible assets. The event-driven deemed cost exemption, however, is broader in scope because it specifies that when a first-time adopter established a deemed cost in accordance with previous GAAP for some or all of its assets and liabilities by measuring them at their fair value at one particular date because of an event such as a privatisation or initial public offering, the entity is allowed to use such event-driven fair value measurements as deemed cost for IFRS accounting standards at the date of that measurement. Certain limitations in the scope of the exemption are described below. Although a first-time adopter could use an event-driven fair value measurement as deemed cost for any asset or liability, it does not have to use them for all assets and liabilities that were revalued as a result of the event. There are two important limitations in the scope of this exemption: • While it applies, in principle, to all assets and liabilities of an entity, it does not override the recognition criteria in IFRS accounting standards (see section 3.2 above). [IFRS 1.10]. Consequently, a first-time adopter derecognises goodwill, assets (e.g., certain intangible assets such as brand names and research) and liabilities that do not qualify for recognition under IFRS accounting standards in the statement of financial position of the entity. [IFRS 1.IG50]. • It cannot be used if the event-driven revaluation did not result in a re-measurement to full fair value (i.e., it cannot be used in the case of a partial step-up towards fair value). If revaluations under previous GAAP did not satisfy the criteria in IFRS 1.D6 (see section 5.4.1.A above) or IFRS 1.D8 (see above), the accounting options available for those previously revalued assets that are property, plant and equipment, right-of-use assets, intangible assets and investment property accounted using cost model are the same as those explained in section 5.4.1.A above. 5.4.2.A ‘Push down’ accounting Under some previous GAAPs, an entity might have prepared its financial statements using ‘push down’ accounting, that is, the carrying amount of its assets and liabilities is based on their fair value at the date it became a subsidiary of its parent. If such a subsidiary subsequently adopts IFRS accounting standards, it will often require a very significant effort to determine the carrying amount of those assets and liabilities on a historical cost basis at the date of transition. The event-driven deemed cost exemption (see section 5.4.2 above) applies to events ‘such as a privatisation or initial public offering.’ [IFRS 1.D8]. This list of events is clearly not meant to be exhaustive, but rather describes events that result in re-measurement of some or all assets and liabilities at their fair value. First-time adoption How we see it An acquisition that results in an entity becoming a subsidiary is a ‘change of control event’ similar to a privatisation or an initial public offering. In our view, the application of ‘push down’ accounting results in event-driven fair value measurements that could be used as deemed cost for IFRS accounting standards at the date of that measurement. The exemption can only be used, however, if ‘push down’ accounting resulted in the recognition of the related assets and liabilities at their fair value. For example, previous GAAP might not have required remeasurement to full fair value in the case of a partial acquisition or a step-acquisition, or if there was a bargain purchase that was allocated, for example, to reduce the fair values of long-lived assets. How we see it In these cases, the entity would not qualify for the event-driven deemed cost exemption, since the event did not result in the measurement of its assets and liabilities at their fair value. 5.4.2.B ‘Fresh start’ accounting Some previous GAAPs require an entity that emerges from bankruptcy or undertakes a legal reorganisation to apply ‘fresh start’ accounting, which involves recognition of assets and liabilities at their fair value at that date. How we see it In our view, the application of ‘fresh start’ accounting results in an event-driven fair value measurement (see section 5.4.2 above) that could be used as deemed cost for IFRS accounting standards at the date of that measurement. The use of the exemption is limited to instances that resulted in the recognition of the related assets and liabilities at their full fair value (i.e., it cannot be used in the case of a partial step-up towards fair value). 5.4.2.C Exemption for event-driven revaluations after the date of transition The event-driven revaluation exemption allows a first-time adopter to recognise in its first IFRS financial statements fair values arising from events whose measurement date is after the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards but during the periods covered by the first IFRS financial statements. The event-driven fair value measurements are recognised as deemed cost at the date that the event occurs. An entity recognises the resulting adjustments directly in retained earnings (or if appropriate, another category of equity) at the measurement date. [IFRS 1.D8(b)]. The Board explicitly considered whether to allow a first-time adopter that uses a revaluation subsequent to the date of transition to ‘work back’ to the deemed cost on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards by adjusting the revaluation amounts to exclude any depreciation, amortisation or impairment between the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and the date of that measurement. The Board rejected this approach “because making such adjustments would require hindsight and the computed carrying amounts on the date of transition to IFRSs would be neither the historical costs of the revalued assets nor their fair values on that date”. [IFRS 1.BC46B]. Accordingly, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the entity either establishes the deemed cost by applying the criteria in IFRS 1.D5-D7 (see section 5.4.1 above) or measures assets and liabilities under the other requirements in IFRS 1. [IFRS 1.D8(b)]. 5.4.3 Deemed cost for oil and gas assets It is common practice in some countries to account for exploration and development costs for oil and gas properties in development or production phases in cost centres that include all properties in a large geographical area, e.g., under the ‘full cost accounting’ method. However, this method of accounting generally uses a unit of account that is much larger than that which is acceptable under IFRS accounting standards. Applying IFRS accounting standards fully retrospectively would pose significant problems for first-time adopters because it would also require amortisation at the unit of account level (determined in accordance with IFRS accounting standards) to be calculated (on a unit of production basis) for each year, using a reserves base that has changed over time because of changes in factors such as geological understanding and prices for oil and gas. In many cases, particularly for older assets, this information may not be available. Even when such information is available, the effort and cost to determine the opening balances at the date of transition would usually be very high. [IFRS 1.BC47A]. First-time adoption For these entities, use of the fair value as deemed cost exemption (see section 5.4.1 above) was not considered to be suitable because: [IFRS 1.BC47B] “Determining the fair value of oil and gas assets is a complex process that begins with the difficult task of estimating the volume of reserves and resources. When the fair value amounts must be audited, determining significant inputs to the estimates generally requires the use of qualified external experts. For entities with many oil and gas assets, the use of this fair value as deemed cost alternative would not meet the Board’s stated intention of avoiding excessive cost …”. The IASB therefore decided to grant an exemption for a first-time adopter that accounted under its previous GAAP for “exploration and development costs for oil and gas properties in the development or production phases … in cost centres that include all properties in a large geographical area”. [IFRS 1.D8A]. Under the exemption, a first-time adopter could elect to measure oil and gas assets at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards on the following basis: [IFRS 1.D8A] (a) Exploration and evaluation assets at the amount determined under the entity’s previous GAAP. (b) Assets in the development or production phases at the amount determined for the cost centre under the entity’s previous GAAP. This amount must be allocated to the cost centre’s underlying assets pro rata using reserve volumes or reserve values as of that date. For this purpose, ‘oil and gas assets’ comprise only those assets used in the exploration, evaluation, development or production of oil and gas. [IFRS 1.D8A]. A first-time adopter that uses the exemption under (b) above discloses that fact and the basis on which carrying amounts determined under previous GAAP were allocated (see section 6.5.3 below). [IFRS 1.31A]. To avoid the use of deemed costs resulting in an oil and gas asset being measured at more than its recoverable amount, the Board also decided that oil and gas assets that were valued using this exemption must be tested for impairment at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards as follows: **[IFRS 1.D8A, IFRS 1.BC47D]** • Exploration and evaluation assets must be tested for impairment under IFRS 6 • Assets in the development and production phases must be tested for impairment under IAS 36 The deemed cost amounts are reduced to take account of any impairment charge. [IFRS 1.D8A]. Finally, a first-time adopter that applies the deemed cost exemption for oil and gas assets in the development or production phases in (b) above must also apply IFRS 1.D21A to the related decommissioning and restoration obligation (see section 5.12.2 below). This is required instead of applying IFRS 1.D21 (see section 5.12.1 below) or IFRIC 1 Changes in Existing Decommissioning, Restoration and Similar Liabilities. [IFRS 1.D21A]. First-time adoption Practical example 5-6 below presents disclosure of the use of the ‘deemed cost for oil and gas assets’ exemption. Practical example 5-6: Zargon Oil & Gas Ltd. (2011) Canada ## ANNUAL FINANCIAL REPORT [extract] ## Contents - 27. RECONCILIATION OF TRANSITION FROM CANADIAN GAAP TO IFRS [extract] NOTES TO THE CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] Explanatory notes [extract] … 5.4.4 Deemed cost for assets used (or previously used) in operations subject to rate regulation Entities that hold items of property, plant and equipment, right-of-use assets under IFRS 16, or intangible assets that are used, or were previously used, in operations subject to rate regulation might have capitalised, as part of the carrying amounts, amounts that do not qualify for capitalisation in accordance with IFRS accounting standards. The Basis for Conclusions to IFRS 1 notes that “For example, when setting rates regulators often permit entities to capitalise, …, an allowance for the cost of financing the asset’s acquisition, construction or production. This allowance typically includes an imputed cost of equity. IFRSs do not permit an entity to capitalise an imputed cost of equity.” [IFRS 1.BC47F]. First-time adoption The IASB decided to permit a first-time adopter with operations subject to rate regulation to elect to use the previous GAAP carrying amount of such an item at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards as deemed cost. [IFRS 1.D8B]. In the Board’s view, this exemption is consistent with other exemptions in IFRS 1 in that it “avoids excessive costs while meeting the objectives of the IFRS”. [IFRS 1.BC47I]. Operations are subject to rate regulation if they are governed by a framework for establishing the prices that can be charged to customers for goods or services and that framework is subject to oversight and/or approval by a rate regulator (as defined in IFRS 14). [IFRS 1.D8B, IFRS 14 Appendix A]. Extract from IFRS 1 D8B Some entities hold items of property, plant and equipment, right-of-use assets or intangible assets that are used, or were previously used, in operations subject to rate regulation. The carrying amount of such items might include amounts that were determined under previous GAAP but do not qualify for capitalisation in accordance with IFRSs. If this is the case, a first-time adopter may elect to use the previous GAAP carrying amount of such an item at the date of transition to IFRSs as deemed cost. If an entity applies this exemption to an item, it need not apply it to all items. At the date of transition to IFRSs, an entity shall test for impairment in accordance with IAS 36 each item for which this exemption is used. For the purposes of this paragraph, operations are subject to rate regulation if they are governed by a framework for establishing the prices that can be charged to customers for goods or services and that framework is subject to oversight and/or approval by a rate regulator (as defined in IFRS 14 Regulatory Deferral Accounts). Without this exemption, a first-time adopter with operations subject to rate regulation would have had either to restate those items retrospectively to remove the non-qualifying amounts, or to use fair value as deemed cost (see section 5.4.1 above). Both alternatives, the Board reasoned, pose significant practical challenges, the cost of which can outweigh the benefits. [IFRS 1.BC47G]. Typically, once amounts are included in the total cost of an item of property, plant and equipment, they are no longer tracked separately. Therefore, their removal would require historical information that, given the typical age of some of the assets involved, is probably no longer available and would be difficult to estimate. For many of these assets, it might be impractical to use the exemption to use a deemed cost equal to transition-date fair value (IFRS 1.D5) as such information may not be readily available. [IFRS 1.BC47H]. A first-time adopter that applies this exemption to an item need not apply it to all items. At the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the first-time adopter must test for impairment in accordance with IAS 36 each item for which it used the exemption. [IFRS 1.D8B]. If a first-time adopter uses the exemption for assets used in operations subject to rate regulation, it must disclose that fact and the basis on which carrying amounts were determined under previous GAAP (see section 6.5.4 below). [IFRS 1.31B]. ## Contents - 5. First Time Adoption of IFRS: [extract] First-time adoption Practical example 5-7 below illustrates disclosure of the use of the deemed cost exemption for property, plant and equipment and intangible assets used in operations subject to rate regulation although IFRS 14 is not applied. Practical example 5-7: Enersource Corporation (2012) Canada Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] While the current exemption in IFRS 1.D8B provides a one-time relief to determine the transition-date balances of the eligible property, plant and equipment, right-of-use assets and intangible assets, IFRS 14 is wider in scope (see section 5.22 below). First-time adoption 5.4.5 Summary At its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, a first-time adopter is allowed under IFRS 1 to measure the following assets and liabilities using a deemed cost: (a) An item of property, plant and equipment, an investment property, a right-of-use asset and an intangible asset meeting certain criteria: (i) At fair value at the date of transition (see section 5.4.1 above) [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D7] (ii) Using a previous GAAP revaluation amount (at or before the date of transition) that is broadly comparable to (see section 5.4.1 above): [IFRS 1.D6] • Fair value at the date of revaluation Or • Cost or depreciated cost under IFRS accounting standards adjusted to reflect, for example, changes in a general or specific price index as a deemed cost as at the date of the revaluation (b) Some or all of its assets and liabilities, based on a previous GAAP measure of deemed cost at one particular date that arose from an event-driven fair value, for example, at the date of an initial public offering or privatisation (see section 5.4.2 above) [IFRS 1.D8] (c) Certain oil and gas assets at a deemed cost at the date of transition based on the previous GAAP carrying amount and tested for impairment as at the date of transition (see section 5.4.3 above) [IFRS 1.D8A] (d) Certain assets used or previously used in operations subject to rate regulation, at a deemed cost at the date of transition based on the previous GAAP carrying amount and tested for impairment as at the date of transition (see section 5.4.4 above) [IFRS 1.D8B] (e) Assets acquired, liabilities assumed and goodwill in a business combination not restated under IFRS 3 (see section 5.2 above): (i) Using a deemed cost, as at the date of the business combination, equal to the carrying amount under previous GAAP immediately after that business combination [IFRS 1.C4(e)] (ii) If the item was not recognised under previous GAAP, using the carrying amount at the date of transition on the basis that IFRS accounting standards would require in the separate statement of financial position of the acquiree [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(f)] (iii) Goodwill for an unconsolidated subsidiary under previous GAAP which was acquired in a past business combination at the difference at the date of transition between the parent’s interest in those adjusted carrying amounts and the cost in the parent’s separate financial statements of its investment in the subsidiary [IFRS 1.C4(j)] (f) A right-of-use asset for a lessee, at a carrying amount based on certain exemptions in IFRS 1 (see section 5.5 below) [IFRS 1.D9, IFRS 1.D9B, IFRS 1.D9C, IFRS 1.D9D, IFRS 1.D9E] (g) An investment in a subsidiary, joint venture or associate in separate financial statements (see section 5.7.2 below) [IFRS 1.D15(b)] The fact that IFRS 1 offers so many different bases for measurement does not disturb the IASB as it reasons that “cost is generally equivalent to fair value at the date of acquisition. Therefore, the use of fair value as the deemed cost of an asset means that an entity will report the same cost data as if it had acquired an asset with the same remaining service potential at the date of transition to IFRSs. If there is any lack of comparability, it arises from the aggregation of costs incurred at different dates, rather than from the targeted use of fair value as deemed cost for some assets. The Board regarded this approach as justified to solve the unique problem of introducing IFRSs in a cost-effective way without damaging transparency.” [IFRS 1.BC43]. Although this is valid, it still means that an individual first-time adopter can greatly influence its future reported performance by carefully making decisions on whether to apply these exemptions for the valuation of its assets. Users of the financial statements of a first-time adopter are encouraged therefore to be mindful that historical trends under the previous GAAP might no longer be present in an entity’s IFRS financial statements. Where IFRS accounting standards require subsequent measurement of an asset or liability on a basis that is not based on original cost, for example, fair value, the asset or liability must be measured on that basis as at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.7]. This does not mean that a deemed cost could not be used. For example, where a class of property, plant and equipment is measured using the revaluation model, its carrying amount reflects the revalued amount under IAS 16 but the revaluation surplus is based on the difference between the revalued amount and the deemed cost (or historical cost) as at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (see section 7.4.3 below). First-time adoption 5.5 Leases IFRS 1 (paragraphs D9-D9E) provide first-time adopters with some exemptions from full retrospective application of IFRS 16. [IFRS 1.D9, IFRS 1.D9B, IFRS 1.D9C, IFRS 1.D9D, IFRS 1.D9E]. Firstly, a first-time adopter is allowed to assess whether a contract existing at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards contains a lease by applying IFRS 16.9-11 to these contracts on the basis of facts and circumstances existing at that date. [IFRS 1.D9, IFRS 16.9, IFRS 16.10, IFRS 16.11]. Illustration 5-21 below demonstrates how a first-time adopter determines whether a contractual arrangement contains a lease. Illustration 5-21: Determining whether an arrangement contains a lease Entity A’s first IFRS financial statements are for a period that ends on 31 December 20X3 and include comparative information for 20X2 only. Its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. On 1 January 20X0, Entity A entered into a take-or-pay arrangement to supply gas. On 1 January 20X1, there was a change in the contractual terms of the arrangement. On 1 January 20X2, Entity A could determine whether the arrangement contains a lease under IFRS 16 on the basis of facts and circumstances existing on that date. Alternatively, Entity A could apply the criteria in IFRS 16 on the basis of facts and circumstances existing on 1 January 20X0 but will need to reassess the arrangement on 1 January 20X1 in respect of the changes made on that date. [IFRS 16.11]. IFRS 1 allows a first-time adopter that is a lessee to apply the following approach to all of its leases (subject to the practical expedients described in IFRS 1.D9D, which are discussed further below). [IFRS 1.D9B, IFRS 1.D9C, IFRS 1.D9D]. Extract from IFRS 1 D9B When a first-time adopter that is a lessee recognises lease liabilities and right-of-use assets, it may apply the following approach to all of its leases (subject to the practical expedients described in paragraph D9D): (a) measure a lease liability at the date of transition to IFRSs. A lessee following this approach shall measure that lease liability at the present value of the remaining lease payments (see paragraph D9E), discounted using the lessee’s incremental borrowing rate (see paragraph D9E) at the date of transition to IFRSs. (b) measure a right-of-use asset at the date of transition to IFRSs. The lessee shall choose, on a lease-by-lease basis, to measure that right-of-use asset at either: (i) its carrying amount as if IFRS 16 had been applied since the commencement date of the lease (see paragraph D9E), but discounted using the lessee’s incremental borrowing rate at the date of transition to IFRSs; or (ii) an amount equal to the lease liability, adjusted by the amount of any prepaid or accrued lease payments relating to that lease recognised in the statement of financial position immediately before the date of transition to IFRSs. (c) apply IAS 36 to right-of-use assets at the date of transition to IFRSs. D9C Notwithstanding the requirements in paragraph D9B, a first-time adopter that is a lessee shall measure the right-of-use asset at fair value at the date of transition to IFRSs for leases that meet the definition of investment property in IAS 40 and are measured using the fair value model in IAS 40 from the date of transition to IFRSs. Additionally, a first-time adopter that is a lessee is allowed to use one or more of the following practical expedients at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (applied on a lease-by-lease basis): [IFRS 1.D9D] (a) Apply a single discount rate to a portfolio of leases with reasonably similar characteristics (e.g., a similar remaining lease term for a similar class of underlying asset in a similar economic environment). (b) Elect not to apply IFRS 1.D9B to leases for which the lease term ends within 12 months of the date of transition. Instead, the entity must account for (including giving disclosure of information about) these leases as if they were short-term leases accounted for under IFRS 16.6. [IFRS 1.D9B, IFRS 16.6]. (c) Elect not to apply IFRS 1.D9B to leases for which the underlying asset is of low value (as described in IFRS 16.B3-B8). Instead, the entity must account for (including giving disclosure of information about) these leases under IFRS 16.6. (see Chapter 24 at section 5.1.2). [IFRS 1.D9B, IFRS 16.6, IFRS 16.B3-B8]. (d) Exclude initial direct costs from the measurement of the right-of-use asset at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. (e) Use hindsight, such as in determining the lease term if the contract contains options to extend or terminate the lease. Lease payments, lessee, lessee’s incremental borrowing rate, commencement date of the lease, initial direct costs and lease term are defined in IFRS 16 and are used in IFRS 1 with the same meaning (see Chapter 24 at section 4 and Appendix C). [IFRS 1.D9E, IFRS 16 Appendix A]. First-time adoption 5.6 Cumulative translation differences IAS 21 requires that an entity recognise some translation differences in other comprehensive income and accumulate these in a separate component of equity. On disposal of a foreign operation, the cumulative amount of the exchange differences deferred in the separate component of equity relating to that foreign operation (which includes, for example, the cumulative translation difference for that foreign operation, the exchange differences arising on certain translations to a different presentation currency and any gains and losses on related hedges) must be reclassified to profit or loss when the gain or loss on disposal is recognised. This also applies to exchange differences arising on monetary items that form part of a reporting entity’s net investment in a foreign operation in its consolidated financial statements. [IAS 21.32, **IAS 21.39, IAS 21.48, IFRS 1.D12].** Full retrospective application of IAS 21 would require a first-time adopter to restate all financial statements of its foreign operations to IFRS accounting standards from their date of inception or later acquisition onwards, and then determine the cumulative translation differences arising in relation to each of these foreign operations. A first-time adopter need not comply with these requirements for cumulative translation differences that existed at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. If it uses this exemption: [IFRS 1.D13] (a) The cumulative translation differences for all foreign operations are deemed to be zero at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (b) The gain or loss on a subsequent disposal of any foreign operation must exclude translation differences that arose before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards but must include later translation differences How we see it If a first-time adopter chooses to use this exemption, it must apply it to all foreign operations at its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, which include any foreign operations that became first-time adopters before their parent notwithstanding that IFRS 1.D17 is applied (see section 5.8.2 below). Any existing separate component of the first-time adopter’s equity relating to such translation differences would be transferred to retained earnings at the date of transition. A subsidiary, an associate or a joint venture which becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent (or investor or venturer) is also allowed to use this exemption even if IFRS 1.D16(a) is applied (see section 5.8.1 below) but has a further option in IFRS 1.D13A as described in section 5.8.1.A below. An entity is allowed to present its financial statements in a presentation currency that differs from its functional currency. IFRS 1 is silent on whether the cumulative translation differences exemption is applied to all translation differences or possibly separately to differences between the parent’s functional currency and the presentation currency. How we see it In our view, IAS 21 does not distinguish between the translation differences arising on translation of subsidiaries into the functional currency of the parent and those arising on the translation from the parent’s functional currency to the presentation currency, therefore, the exemption is applied consistently to both types of translation differences. Since there is no requirement to justify the use of the exemption on grounds of impracticality or undue cost or effort, [IFRS 1.BC55], an entity that already has a separate component of equity and the necessary information to determine how much of it relates to each foreign operation in accordance with IAS 21 (or can do so without much effort) is still able to use the exemption. Accordingly, an entity that has cumulative exchange losses in respect of foreign operations could consider it advantageous to use the exemption if it wishes to avoid having to recognise these losses in profit or loss if the foreign operation is sold at some time in the future. ## Contents - 34. First-time adoption [extract] First-time adoption Practical example 5-8 below illustrates disclosure of the use of this exemption. Practical example 5-8: Sony Group Corporation (2022) Japan Form 20-F [extract] Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] (1) Exemption under IFRS 1 [extract] (4) Notes to reconciliation [extract] … 5.6.1 Gains and losses arising on related hedges IFRS 1 is not entirely clear whether this exemption extends to similar gains and losses arising on related hedges. Paragraph D13, which contains the exemption, explains that a first-time adopter need not comply with ‘these requirements.’ [IFRS 1.D13]. The requirements referred to are those summarised in IFRS 1.D12 which explain that IAS 21 requires an entity: [IFRS 1.D12] (a) To recognise some translation differences in other comprehensive income and accumulate these in a separate component of equity And (b) On disposal of a foreign operation, to reclassify the cumulative translation difference for that foreign operation (including, if applicable, gains and losses on related hedges) from equity to profit or loss as part of the gain or loss on disposal The problem arises because IFRS 1.D12 does not refer to the recognition of hedging gains or losses in other comprehensive income and accumulation in a separate component of equity (only the subsequent reclassification thereof). Accordingly, a very literal reading of the standard might suggest that an entity is required to identify historical gains and losses on such hedges. However, even if this position is accepted, the basis on which this might be done is not at all clear. How we see it We believe it is entirely appropriate for this exemption to be applied to net investment hedges as well as the underlying gains and losses. It is clear that the reasons cited by the IASB for including this exemption, apply as much to related hedges as they do to the underlying exchange differences. First-time adoption 5.7 Investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates 5.7.1 Consolidated financial statements: subsidiaries and structured entities A first-time adopter must consolidate all subsidiaries (as defined in IFRS 10) unless IFRS 10 requires otherwise. [IFRS 1.IG26]. First-time adoption of IFRS accounting standards could therefore result in the consolidation for the first time of a subsidiary not consolidated under previous GAAP, either because the subsidiary was not regarded as such before, or because the parent did not prepare consolidated financial statements. If a first-time adopter did not consolidate a subsidiary under its previous GAAP, it must recognise the assets and liabilities of that subsidiary in its consolidated financial statements at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards at either: [IFRS 1.IG27(a)] (a) If the subsidiary has adopted IFRS accounting standards, the same carrying amounts as in the IFRS financial statements of the subsidiary, after adjusting for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in which it acquired the subsidiary [IFRS 1.D17] Or (b) If the subsidiary has not adopted IFRS accounting standards, the carrying amounts that IFRS accounting standards would require in the subsidiary’s statement of financial position [IFRS 1.C4(j)] If the newly-consolidated subsidiary was acquired in a business combination before the date of the parent’s transition to IFRS accounting standards, goodwill is the difference between the parent’s interest in the carrying amount determined under either (a) or (b) above and the cost in the parent’s separate financial statements of its investment in the subsidiary. This is no more than a pragmatic ‘plug’ that facilitates the consolidation process but does not represent the true goodwill that might have been recorded if IFRS accounting standards had been applied to the original business combination (see section 5.2.7 above). [IFRS 1.C4(j), IFRS 1.IG27(b)]. How we see it If the first-time adopter accounted for the investment as an associate under its previous GAAP, it cannot use the notional goodwill previously calculated under the equity method as the basis for goodwill under IFRS accounting standards. If the parent did not acquire the subsidiary, but established it, it does not recognise goodwill. [IFRS 1.IG27(c)]. Any difference between the cost of investment in the subsidiary in the parent’s separate financial statements and the parent’s interest in the identifiable net assets as determined in (a) or (b) above would be treated as an adjustment to retained earnings, representing the accumulated profits or losses attributable to the parent that would have been recognised as if the subsidiary had always been consolidated. The adjustment of the carrying amounts of assets and liabilities of a first-time adopter’s subsidiaries might affect non-controlling interests and deferred tax, as discussed in section 5.2.9 above. [IFRS 1.C4(k), IFRS 1.IG28]. 5.7.2 Separate financial statements: Cost of an investment in a subsidiary, joint venture or associate When an entity prepares separate financial statements, IAS 27 requires a first-time adopter to account for its investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates either: [IFRS 1.D14] • At cost • In accordance with IFRS 9 Or • Using the equity method as described in IAS 28 (see Chapter 9 at section 2) However, if a first-time adopter measures such an investment at cost then it can elect to measure that investment at one of the following amounts in its separate opening IFRS statement of financial position: [IFRS 1.D15] (a) Cost determined in accordance with IAS 27 Or (b) Deemed cost, which is its: (i) Fair value at the entity’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards in its separate financial statements Or (ii) Previous GAAP carrying amount at that date First-time adoption A first-time adopter is permitted to choose to use either of these bases to measure its investment in each subsidiary, joint venture or associate where it elects to use a deemed cost. [IFRS 1.D15]. A first-time adopter that applies the exemption discloses certain additional information in its financial statements (see section 6.5.2 below). [IFRS 1.31]. For a first-time adopter that chooses to account for such an investment using the equity method procedures in accordance with IAS 28: [IFRS 1.D15A] (a) The first-time adopter applies the exemption for past business combinations in IFRS 1 (Appendix C) to the acquisition of the investment (see section 5.2 above) [IFRS 1 Appendix C] (b) If the entity becomes a first-time adopter for its separate financial statements earlier than for its consolidated financial statements and: (i) Later than its parent, the entity would apply IFRS 1.D16 in its separate financial statements (see section 5.8.1 below). [IFRS 1.D16]. (ii) Later than its subsidiary, the entity would apply IFRS 1.D17 in its separate financial statements (see section 5.8.2 below). [IFRS 1.D17]. 5.8 Assets and liabilities of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures Within groups, some subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures could have a different date of transition to IFRS accounting standards than the parent, investor or venturer, for example, because national legislation required the use of IFRS accounting standards after, or prohibited the use of IFRS accounting standards at, the date of transition of the parent, investor or venturer. As this could have resulted in permanent differences between the carrying amounts reported under IFRS accounting standards in a subsidiary’s own financial statements and those it reports to its parent, the IASB introduced a special exemption regarding the assets and liabilities of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures. IFRS 1 contains detailed guidance on the approach to be adopted when a parent adopts IFRS accounting standards before its subsidiary (see section 5.8.1 below) and when a subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting standards before its parent (see section 5.8.2 below). These provisions also apply when IFRS accounting standards are adopted at different dates by an investor in an associate and the associate, or by a venturer in a joint venture and the joint venture. [IFRS 1.D16, IFRS 1.D17]. In the discussion that follows ‘parent’ includes an investor in an associate or a venturer in a joint venture, and ‘subsidiary’ includes an associate or a joint venture. References to consolidation adjustments include similar adjustments made when applying equity accounting. IFRS 1 also addresses the requirements for a parent that adopts IFRS accounting standards at different dates for the purposes of its consolidated and its separate financial statements (see section 5.8.4 below). 5.8.1 Subsidiary becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent Extract from IFRS 1 D16 If a subsidiary becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent, the subsidiary shall, in its financial statements, measure its assets and liabilities at either: (a) the carrying amounts that would be included in the parent’s consolidated financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRSs, if no adjustments were made for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in which the parent acquired the subsidiary (this election is not available to a subsidiary of an investment entity, as defined in IFRS 10, that is required to be measured at fair value through profit or loss); or (b) the carrying amounts required by the rest of this IFRS, based on the subsidiary’s date of transition to IFRSs. These carrying amounts could differ from those described in (a): (i) when the exemptions in this IFRS result in measurements that depend on the date of transition to IFRSs. (ii) when the accounting policies used in the subsidiary’s financial statements differ from those in the consolidated financial statements. For example, the subsidiary may use as its accounting policy the cost model in IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment, whereas the group may use the revaluation model. A similar election is available to an associate or joint venture that becomes a first-time adopter later than an entity that has significant influence or joint control over it. First-time adoption A subsidiary that becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent therefore has the option to measure its assets and liabilities at the carrying amounts included in the parent’s consolidated financial statements if no adjustments were made for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in which the parent acquired the subsidiary. [IFRS 1.D16(a)]. The Board provided this exemption so that a subsidiary would not have to keep two parallel sets of accounting records based on different dates of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.BC55A]. If a subsidiary does not take advantage of the exemption, it applies IFRS 1, based on its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.D16(b)]. A subsidiary applying IFRS 1.D16(b) would prepare its own IFRS financial statements, completely ignoring the IFRS 1 elections that its parent used when it adopted IFRS accounting standards for its consolidated financial statements. How we see it IFRS 1 does not elaborate on exactly what constitutes ‘consolidation procedures’, for the purposes of IFRS 1.D16(a) but, in our view, it would encompass adjustments required in order to harmonise a subsidiary’s accounting policies with those of the group, as well as purely ‘mechanical’ consolidation adjustments such as the elimination of intragroup balances, profits and losses. Illustration 5-22 below, which is based on the implementation guidance in IFRS 1, demonstrates how an entity applies these requirements. [IFRS 1.IG Example 8]. Illustration 5-22: Parent adopts IFRS accounting standards before subsidiary Entity A presents its (consolidated) first IFRS financial statements in 20X0. Its foreign subsidiary B, wholly owned by Entity A since formation, prepares information under IFRS accounting standards for internal consolidation purposes from that date, but Subsidiary B will not present its first IFRS financial statements until 20X3. If Subsidiary B applies IFRS 1.D16(a), the carrying amounts of its assets and liabilities are the same in both its opening IFRS statement of financial position at 1 January 20X2 and Entity A’s consolidated statement of financial position (except for adjustments for consolidation procedures) and are based on Entity A’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. Alternatively, Subsidiary B is allowed to apply IFRS 1.D16(b) and measure all its assets or liabilities based on its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (1 January 20X2). However, the fact that Subsidiary B becomes a first-time adopter in 20X3 does not change the carrying amounts of its assets and liabilities in Entity A’s consolidated financial statements. Under IFRS 1.D16(a), the numbers in a subsidiary’s IFRS financial statements will be as close to those used by its parent as possible. However, differences other than those arising from business combinations will still exist in many cases, for example: • A subsidiary might have hedged an exposure by entering into a transaction with a fellow subsidiary. Such a transaction could qualify for hedge accounting in the subsidiary’s own financial statements but not in the group’s consolidated financial statements. • A pension plan might have to be classified as a defined contribution plan from the subsidiary’s point of view, but is accounted for as a defined benefit plan in the group’s consolidated financial statements. The IASB seems content with the fact that the exemption will ease some practical problems, [IFRS 1.BC62], though it will rarely succeed in achieving more than a moderate reduction of the number of reconciling differences between a subsidiary’s own reporting and the numbers used by its parent. How we see it Importantly, the choice of IFRS 1.D16(a) prevents the subsidiary from electing to apply any of the other voluntary exemptions offered by IFRS 1, since the parent had already made the choices for the group at its date of transition. Therefore, use of IFRS 1.D16(a) might not be appropriate for a subsidiary that prefers to use a different exemption (e.g., fair value as deemed cost) for property, plant and equipment due, for example, to a tax reporting advantage. However, in our view, application of IFRS 1.D16(a) would allow the subsidiary to adopt a new IFRS accounting standard that becomes effective during any of the periods presented in the same manner it was adopted by the parent. First-time adoption Illustration 5-23 below explains the interaction between IFRS 1.D16(a) and the business combination exemptions in Appendix C of IFRS 1 applied by its parent. Illustration 5-23: Interaction between IFRS 1.D16(a) and the business combination exemptions applied by its parent Entity D has been a subsidiary of Entity C since 20X0. Entity E was acquired by Entity D in 20X2 and Entity D accounted for the acquisition under its previous GAAP. Entity C transitioned from previous GAAP to IFRS accounting standards with a date of transition of 1 January 20X3 and decided to apply the business combination exemption in Appendix C of IFRS 1 to this acquisition. Entity D adopted IFRS accounting standards from its date of transition of 1 January 20X6 and chose to apply IFRS 1.D16(a). Can Entity D apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to the acquisition of Entity E in its first IFRS financial statements? How we see it In our view, Entity D cannot apply IFRS 3 to the acquisition of Entity E because, applying IFRS 1.D16(a), a subsidiary which becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent measures its assets and liabilities at the carrying amounts that would be included in the parent’s consolidated financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. Therefore, Entity D cannot apply IFRS 3 retrospectively, meeting the primary objective of using the IFRS 1.D16(a) election in keeping one set of books. A subsidiary might become a first-time adopter later than its parent, because it previously prepared a reporting package under IFRS accounting standards for consolidation purposes but did not present a full set of financial statements under IFRS accounting standards. In such circumstances, IFRS 1.D16 still applies. The above election of IFRS 1.D16(a) could be relevant not only when a subsidiary’s reporting package complies fully with the recognition and measurement requirements of IFRS accounting standards, but also when it is adjusted centrally for matters such as review of events after the reporting period and central allocation of pension costs. Adjustments made centrally to an unpublished reporting package are not considered to be corrections of errors for the purposes of the disclosure requirements in IFRS 1. [IFRS 1.26]. However, a subsidiary is not permitted to ignore misstatements that are immaterial to the consolidated financial statements of the group but material to its own financial statements. [IFRS 1.IG31]. How we see it If a subsidiary was acquired after the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards then it cannot apply IFRS 1.D16(a) because there are no carrying amounts included in the parent’s consolidated financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition. Therefore, the subsidiary is unable to use the values recognised in the parent’s consolidated financial statements when it was acquired, since push-down of the group’s purchase accounting values is not allowed in the subsidiary’s financial statements. However, if the subsidiary had recognised those amounts in its previous GAAP financial statements, it could use the same amounts as deemed costs under IFRS accounting standards pursuant to the ‘event-driven’ deemed cost exemption (see section 5.4.2 above). Implementation difficulties in applying IFRS 1.D16(a) might arise if the parent and its subsidiary have different financial years, as explained below. How we see it Application of IFRS 1.D16(a) is more difficult when a parent and its subsidiary have different financial years. In that case, IFRS 1 would seem to require the carrying amounts of the subsidiary’s assets and liabilities under IFRS accounting standards to be based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, which might not even coincide with an interim reporting date of the subsidiary; the same applies to any joint venture or associate. The exemption is also available to associates and joint ventures. This means that in many cases an associate or joint venture that wants to apply IFRS 1.D16(a) will need to choose which shareholder it considers its ‘parent’ for IFRS 1 purposes if more than one investor/joint venturer have already applied IFRS accounting standards. The associate or joint venture will determine the carrying amount of its assets and liabilities under IFRS accounting standards by reference to that ‘parent’s’ date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. A subsidiary that adopts IFRS accounting standards after its investment entity parent is not permitted to use IFRS 1.D16(a) (see section 5.8.5.A below). First-time adoption 5.8.1.A Cumulative translation differences IFRS 1.D13A permits a subsidiary that applies IFRS 1.D16(a) to measure cumulative translation differences for all foreign operations at the carrying amount that would be included in its parent’s consolidated financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (if no adjustments were made for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in which the parent acquired the subsidiary). A similar election is available to an associate or joint venture that uses the exemption in IFRS 1.D16(a). [IFRS 1.D13A, IFRS 1.D16(a)]. The subsidiary still has the option to apply IAS 21 retrospectively or apply IFRS 1.D13 and deem the cumulative translation differences for all foreign operations to be zero at its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.6 above). **[IFRS 1.D12, IFRS 1.D13, IFRS 1.D13A, IFRS 1.BC55C].** 5.8.2 Parent becomes a first-time adopter later than its subsidiary Paragraph D17 of IFRS 1 addresses the situation where a parent becomes a first-time adopter later than its subsidiary. [IFRS 1.D17]. Extract from IFRS 1 D17 However, if an entity becomes a first-time adopter later than its subsidiary (or associate or joint venture) the entity shall, in its consolidated financial statements, measure the assets and liabilities of the subsidiary (or associate or joint venture) at the same carrying amounts as in the financial statements of the subsidiary (or associate or joint venture), after adjusting for consolidation and equity accounting adjustments and for the effects of the business combination in which the entity acquired the subsidiary. Notwithstanding this requirement, a non-investment entity parent shall not apply the exception to consolidation that is used by any investment entity subsidiaries. Similarly, if a parent becomes a first-time adopter for its separate financial statements earlier or later than for its consolidated financial statements, it shall measure its assets and liabilities at the same amounts in both financial statements, except for consolidation adjustments. Unlike other first-time adoption exemptions, this exemption does not offer a choice between different accounting alternatives. [IFRS 1.D17]. In fact, while a subsidiary that adopts IFRS accounting standards later than its parent can choose to prepare its first IFRS financial statements by reference to its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards or that of its parent, the parent itself must use the measurements of the assets and liabilities already used in the subsidiary’s IFRS financial statements, adjusted as appropriate for consolidation procedures and the effects of the business combination in which it acquired the subsidiary. [IFRS 1.BC63]. However, this exemption does not preclude the parent from adjusting the subsidiary’s assets and liabilities for a different accounting policy, e.g., cost model or revaluation model for accounting for property, plant and equipment. The case where a non-investment entity parent adopts IFRS accounting standards after its investment entity subsidiary is discussed in section 5.8.5.B below. Illustration 5-24 below, which is based on the implementation guidance in IFRS 1, demonstrates how an entity applies these requirements. [IFRS 1.IG Example 9]. Illustration 5-24: Subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting standards before parent Entity F presents its (consolidated) first IFRS financial statements in 20X3. Its foreign subsidiary G, wholly owned by Entity F since formation, presented its first IFRS financial statements in 20X0. Until 20X3, Subsidiary G prepared information for internal consolidation purposes under Entity F’s previous GAAP. The carrying amounts of Subsidiary G’s assets and liabilities at 1 January 20X2 (date of transition to IFRS accounting standards for Entity F) are the same in both Entity F’s (consolidated) opening IFRS statement of financial position and Subsidiary G’s own financial statements (except for adjustments for consolidation procedures) and are based on Subsidiary G’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The fact that Entity F becomes a first-time adopter in 20X3 does not change those carrying amounts. [IFRS 1.D17]. First-time adoption How we see it When a subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting standards before its parent, this will limit the parent’s ability to choose first-time adoption exemptions in IFRS 1 freely as related to that subsidiary, as shown in Illustration 5-25 below. However, this does not mean that the parent’s ability to choose first-time adoption exemptions will always be limited. For example, a parent could still deem a subsidiary’s cumulative translation differences to be zero because IFRS 1 specifically states that under the option “the cumulative translation differences for all foreign operations are deemed to be zero at the date of transition to IFRSs” (emphasis added). For a discussion of the various options in respect of cumulative translation differences, see sections 5.6 and 5.8.1.A above. Illustration 5-25: Limited ability to choose first-time adoption exemptions Entity H will adopt IFRS accounting standards for the first time in 20X6 and its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X5. Subsidiary I adopted IFRS accounting standards in 20X1 and its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards was 1 January 20X0: (a) Subsidiary I and Entity H both account for their property, plant and equipment at historical cost under IAS 16 Upon first-time adoption, Entity H is only allowed to adjust carrying amounts of Subsidiary I’s assets and liabilities for the effects of consolidation and the business combination in which Subsidiary I was acquired. Entity H can therefore not apply the exemption to use fair value as deemed cost for Subsidiary I’s property, plant and equipment as at its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (1 January 20X5). (b) Subsidiary I accounts for its property, plant and equipment at revalued amounts under IAS 16, while Entity H accounts for its property, plant and equipment at historical cost under IAS 16 In this case, Entity H would not be allowed to apply the exemption to use fair value as deemed cost of Subsidiary I’s property, plant and equipment because IFRS 1.D17 would only permit adjustments for the effects of consolidation and the business combination in which Subsidiary I was acquired. Although a consolidation adjustment would be necessary, this would only be to adjust Subsidiary I’s revalued amounts to figures based on historical cost. (c) Subsidiary I deemed the cumulative translation difference for all its foreign subsidiaries to be zero at its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (i.e., 1 January 20X0) When Entity H adopts IFRS accounting standards it can deem Subsidiary I’s cumulative translation differences to be zero at its date of transition (1 January 20X5). This is because those cumulative translation differences result from translation of Subsidiary I’s financial statements into Entity H’s presentation currency. 5.8.3 Implementation guidance on accounting for assets and liabilities of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures The requirements of IFRS 1 for a parent and subsidiary with different dates of transition to IFRS accounting standards do not override the following requirements of IFRS 1: [IFRS 1.IG30] • The parent’s election to use the business combinations exemption in Appendix C discussed in section 5.2 above, which applies to assets and liabilities of a subsidiary acquired in a business combination that occurred before the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. However, the rules in IFRS 1.D17, [IFRS 1.D17], summarised in section 5.8.2 above (parent adopting IFRS accounting standards after subsidiary) apply to assets acquired and liabilities assumed by the subsidiary after the business combination and still held and owned by it at the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. • To apply the requirements in IFRS 1 in measuring items in the financial statements for which the provisions in IFRS 1.D16 and D17 are not relevant. [IFRS 1.D16, IFRS 1.D17]. For example, the use of the exemption in IFRS 1.D16(a) to measure assets and liabilities at the carrying amounts in the parent’s consolidated financial statements does not affect the use of the exemption in IFRS 1.D13 to deem the cumulative translation differences for all foreign operations are zero at its date of transition, discussed in section 5.6 above, since this is neither an asset nor a liability. However, the subsidiary also has an option to measure the cumulative translation differences at the carrying amount that would be included in its parent’s consolidated financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (if no adjustments were made for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in which the parent acquired the subsidiary). See section 5.8.1.A above. [IFRS 1.D12, IFRS 1.D13, IFRS 1.D13A]. • A first-time adopter must give all the disclosures required by IFRS 1 as of its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards - see section 6 below. First-time adoption Section 6.8 below discusses the disclosures required where IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures is applied.6 5.8.4 Adoption of IFRS accounting standards on different dates in separate and consolidated financial statements Sometimes, an entity becomes a first-time adopter for its separate financial statements earlier or later than for its consolidated financial statements. Such a situation could arise, for example, when a parent avails itself of the exemption under IFRS 10.4 from preparing consolidated financial statements and prepares its separate financial statements under IFRS accounting standards (see Chapter 7 at section 2.2). [IFRS 10.4]. Subsequently, if the parent ceases to be entitled to the exemption or chooses not to use it, the parent would be required to apply IFRS 1 in its first IFRS consolidated financial statements. Another example might be that, under local law, an entity is required to prepare its consolidated financial statements under IFRS accounting standards, but is required (or permitted) to prepare its separate financial statements under local GAAP. Subsequently, the parent chooses, or is required, to prepare its separate financial statements under IFRS accounting standards. If a parent becomes a first-time adopter for its separate financial statements earlier or later than for its consolidated financial statements, it must measure its assets and liabilities at the same amounts in both financial statements, except for consolidation adjustments. [IFRS 1.D17]. How we see it The requirement is merely that the ‘same’ amounts be used in the first-time adopter’s separate and consolidated financial statements under IFRS accounting standards, without being explicit as to which set of financial statements is used as the benchmark. However, we believe that it is clear from the context that the IASB intends that the measurement basis used in whichever set of financial statements first comply with IFRS accounting standards must also be used when IFRS accounting standards are subsequently adopted in the other set. 5.8.5 Application to investment entities under IFRS 10 IFRS 10 requires a parent that is an ‘investment entity’ as defined in the standard (see Chapter 7 at section 10) to account for most of its subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss in its consolidated financial statements rather than through consolidation. [IFRS 10.31, IFRS 10 Appendix A]. This exception from normal consolidation procedures does not apply to: • A parent entity that owns a subsidiary which is an investment entity but the parent itself is not an investment entity [IFRS 10.33] • A parent that is an investment entity in accounting for a subsidiary that is not itself an investment entity and whose main purpose and activities are providing services that relate to the investment entity’s investment activities [IFRS 10.32] In IFRS 1, there are provisions relating to a parent adopting IFRS accounting standards earlier or later than its subsidiary. Below, we deal with situations where: • A subsidiary that is required to be measured at fair value through profit or loss adopts IFRS accounting standards after its parent which is an investment entity (see section 5.8.5.A below) • A parent that is not an investment entity adopts IFRS accounting standards after a subsidiary which is an investment entity (see section 5.8.5.B below) IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures was issued by the IASB in May 2024 to simplify the preparation of financial statements by eligible subsidiaries. Eligible subsidiaries that elect to apply IFRS 19 are allowed to provide reduced disclosures while still applying the recognition, measurement and presentation requirements in other IFRS accounting standards. IFRS 19 is effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027, with earlier application permitted (subject to jurisdictional endorsement). See Chapter 4 at section 7 for further details on the requirements of IFRS 19. First-time adoption 5.8.5.A Subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting standards after investment entity parent In this case, the subsidiary that is required to be measured at fair value through profit or loss must measure its assets and liabilities under the general provisions of IFRS 1, based on its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (i.e., IFRS 1.D16(b) - see section 5.8.1 above), [IFRS 1.D16(b)], rather than (as would generally be permitted under IFRS 1.D16(a) - see section 5.8.1 above) by reference to the carrying amounts of its assets and liabilities in the IFRS consolidated financial statements of its parent, which are based on the fair value of the investment in the subsidiary.
